Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Here's what that looks like end to end: set up a robot and build real applications.

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Once the robot is live, you can use a simple prompt to create a basic task within just a few minutes:

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Hydrogen's efficiency makes it practical to run a multi-step robot application all day. In this example, a developer uses Hydrogen to build a small web storefront for a soap shop, then connects it to two YAM Pro arms. When an order comes in, the arms set out a packing bin, pick the ordered mint and citrus bars, and pull a printed thank-you note from a printer.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

With just a few prompts, the FR3 Duo simulates a chess board and then can play chess against itself or take on human opponents.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Gemini 4 Argon pushes the ceiling of physical reasoning. Gemini 4 Hydrogen makes those capabilities fast and affordable enough to use every day, and the Antigravity Robotics plugin puts them in a workbench where anyone can go from a robot in a box to a working application. We can't wait to see what you build.

You can share your own creations and use cases on the plugin’s GitHub discussions page. If you have any ideas, custom skills, or derived work you think would be helpful, please let us know—we’ll keep improving the plugins.

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team

Google’s Spark AI agent becomes ‘Tasks’ — and now it’s free

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is rebranding its Spark AI agent as “Tasks” and making it free to use. The feature, which is powered by Google’s new Gemini 4 Hydrogen AI model, can do things like create meal plans, manage your calendar, and dig through your emails to find recurring expenses you might not need.

Users in the US will get access to a few other features, including the ability for the AI agent to call businesses to compare product pricing and check inventory, purchase items with Google Pay, and sign into accounts that support Google Password Manager. US users can also assign Gemini tasks by emailing gemini@google.com or texting it.

Tasks in Gemini is just one of the many agentic AI features that have emerged over the past year, including Claude Duet, OpenAI’s Dots, and Meta’s Muse. Google says Gemini will perform tasks in a “hardware-isolated virtual machine that’s dedicated to your account and never shared with other users.” You can also provide instructions for Gemini while it completes tasks. When performing more sensitive tasks, like purchasing an item, it will stop working and ask for your permission before following through.

/U P D A T E/ - Vertiv Holdings Co/

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the news release, Vertiv Advances 800 VDC Power Architectures to Support Growing AI Data Center Demands, issued 17-Nov-2026 by Vertiv Holdings Co over PR Newswire, we are advised by the company that changes have been made. The complete, corrected release follows, with additional details at the end:

The Vertivâ¢ 800 VDC portfolio spans power conversion, energy storage, distribution and protection, delivering comprehensive power train architectures from the medium-voltage grid to the compute tray

800 VDC portfolio to be showcased at the Vertiv booth at the SC26 International Conference for High-Performance Computing, Networking, Storage, and Analysis

COLUMBUS, Ohio, Nov. 16, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Vertiv Holdings Co. (NYSE: VRT), a global leader in critical digital infrastructure, today added 800 VDC systems for the AI compute pod and the data hall, extending a portfolio that began with the May 2026 introduction of the Vertivâ¢ PowerDirect 5000 Power Rack to three deployment levels: rack, compute pod and data hall. The expanded portfolio enables operators to meet rising power demands in next-generation AI data centers and scale infrastructure as compute requirements evolve.

As AI workloads continue to increase power requirements across the data center, operators are evaluating 800 VDC as a potential path to support higher-density computing environments, but adoption will not follow a single architecture or timeline. AC and DC architectures are expected to coexist, making flexibility in power conversion, distribution and deployment essential.

Operators have different facilities, workloads, business models, risk tolerances, and investment horizons. Vertiv's role is to help operators determine which architecture matches their needs, and its portfolio provides operators with the flexibility to introduce 800 VDC at different points in the power train, aligning deployment with facility requirements without committing an entire data center to a single architecture.

"The 800 VDC power train is not just a new power product or conversion methodology; it is a set of architectures and system designs," said Scott Armul, Chief Product and Technology Officer at Vertiv. "Delivering it takes a comprehensive portfolio that brings together power conversion, energy storage, distribution, protection, controls and safety."

Armul added: "To meet these evolving demands, Vertiv has built a system-level 800 VDC portfolio that gives customers the flexibility to implement 800 VDC across different data center configurations. Every layer, from the medium-voltage grid to the compute tray, is designed to work together, with safety built for DC operation."

The Vertivâ¢ PowerDirect 5000 (Â±400 / 800 VDC) Power Rack sits alongside the IT rack. Configurable up to 800 kW, it accepts facility 480 VAC and delivers 800 VDC or Â±400 VDC. The power rack holds 30 hot-pluggable 30 kW power supply units and an integrated battery backup unit that is designed to provide at least 50 seconds of hold-up time.

The Vertivâ¢ PowerDirect 3000 90 kW, 19" 800 VDC Power Shelf is a 1U DC-DC converter located inside the compute rack. The power shelf converts 800 VDC to 54 VDC for compute trays at up to 98.5% peak efficiency and supports 180% pulse power for 0.5 milliseconds.

Designed for operators adding high-density AI compute to existing facilities, this architecture moves power conversion and energy storage outside the IT rack, freeing 8 to 16 rack units for additional compute. Operators can retain existing upstream AC infrastructure, helping simplify deployment without a facility redesign. With one power rack supporting one or two IT racks, the architecture helps isolate faults and simplify maintenance. The platform supports both 800 VDC and Â±400 VDC through minimal hardware configuration changes, providing flexibility as silicon partners' voltage requirements evolve. It suits brownfield environments, retrofit scenarios and facilities running mixed AC and DC architectures.

The Vertivâ¢ PowerDirect 9000, 1.5 MW 800 VDC Power Center converts 480 VAC to 800 VDC for an entire row or pod. It distributes power via the Vertivâ¢ PowerBar Track DC busway and tap-off boxes to the Vertivâ¢ PowerDirect 3000, 90 kW 19" 800 VDC DC/DC Power Shelves in each rack. The system scales in 60 kW increments through 27 hot-pluggable power supply units. The Vertivâ¢ EnergyCore Li5 800 VDC battery cabinet connects directly to the DC bus and delivers 200 kW of continuous backup at up to 98% conversion efficiency, with up to six cabinets operating in parallel through active current sharing.

Designed for operators deploying megawatt-class AI compute at the pod level, this architecture consolidates power conversion into a single system ahead of the row, reducing component count and eliminating the need for individual power racks alongside each IT rack. It can free additional space for compute and support mixed AC and DC loads within the same facility, suiting colocation providers serving tenants with different workload types and GPU generations. Consolidating power conversion, backup and distribution gives operators coordinated power management across the pod.

The Vertivâ¢ PowerNexus MV SST, 5 MW 800 VDC Solid-State Transformer converts medium-voltage AC directly to 800 VDC in a single conversion stage, feeding seamlessly into the Vertivâ¢ DC busway. It eliminates two to three intermediate conversion steps found in conventional power trains and provides a stable DC output with galvanic isolation. Its outdoor, liquid-cooled and containerized design reduces physical footprint while delivering up to 98% AC-to-DC efficiency and providing fault ride-through and AI power smoothing when connected to external energy storage with a DC/DC converter coupled on the 800 VDC bus. A single unit can support a 4.8 MW super pod reference architecture without chaining. Bidirectional power flow enables improved grid power quality and can provide active power support.

Designed for operators building purpose-built AI factories at campus scale, this architecture simplifies the power train from the medium-voltage connection to the rack. Moving power conversion outdoors can recover valuable indoor space for compute, while fewer conversion stages help reduce cumulative power losses. Battery communication ports coordinate with energy storage systems to manage the rapid load fluctuations of AI workloads. The containerized design also supports faster deployment and standardized replication across campuses.

The Vertivâ¢ 800 VDC portfolio addresses the varying requirements of AI data center deployments through three differentiators:

"Different operators have different requirements, whether they're building new AI facilities, upgrading existing sites, or supporting a mix of tenants and workloads," said Kyle Keeper, Senior Vice President of Vertiv's Power Management Business Unit. "The Vertiv 800 VDC portfolio gives each of them an architecture that fits their needs. Whether deploying at the rack, compute pod or data hall level, customers have the optionality to choose an approach that aligns with their existing infrastructure, deployment goals and power requirements. This flexibility helps them meet today's demands while creating a path to scale as their AI infrastructure evolves."

Vertiv is showcasing its 800 VDC portfolio at SC26, Nov. 17-22, 2026.  Pilot programs, technical validation activities, and collaborative development initiatives tailored to customers' deployment needs are currently underway. Vertiv experts are ready to discuss these solutions and engage on next steps.

For more information on Vertiv's end-to-end portfolio of power and thermal management, infrastructure solutions, IT systems and services for critical digital applications, visit Vertiv.com.

About Vertiv Holdings Co.Vertiv Holdings Co. (NYSE: VRT) brings together hardware, software, analytics and ongoing services to enable its customers' vital applications to run continuously, perform optimally and grow with their business needs. Vertiv solves the most important challenges facing today's data centers, communication networks and commercial and industrial facilities with a portfolio of power, cooling and IT infrastructure solutions and services that extends from the cloud to the edge of the network. Headquartered in Westerville, Ohio, USA, Vertiv does business in more than 130 countries. For more information, and for the latest news and content from Vertiv, visit Vertiv.com.

Python Crypto Backtesting: Reconstructing Point-in-Time Asset Universes to Prevent Survivorship Bias

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclosure: AI assistance was used in preparing this technical article. The code is an illustrative research framework, not a complete exchange-data integration.

A cryptocurrency backtest can produce misleading results even when its price calculations are correct.

If a historical investment universe is constructed from assets that remain active today, cryptocurrencies that previously traded but later disappeared may be excluded from the sample.

The resulting portfolio may look stronger than the strategy could actually have performed at the time.

The solution is to reconstruct the asset universe using information available at each historical decision date.

This is known as point-in-time universe construction.

A useful starting point is a historical asset-status table.

Each record should identify an asset, its listing period, and the period during which it was eligible for trading.

A real dataset should distinguish exchange-specific trading-pair status from the underlying asset's broader existence.

A simple point-in-time universe can be defined as:

An asset with no recorded delisting date remains eligible under this simplified rule.

The resulting universe includes assets that were eligible on January 1, 2022, even if some of them were delisted later.

This is fundamentally different from using today's active-asset list to reconstruct historical portfolios.

Listing status alone does not determine whether an asset should enter a strategy.

A real selection process may also require minimum historical volume, price availability, liquidity, or market capitalization.

The volume filter must use information available at the formation date.

It should not use a future rolling window or a subsequently revised asset ranking.

In production research, point-in-time market capitalization and liquidity data also require careful timestamp handling.

A common data-modeling mistake is to treat delisting as a simple deletion from the asset directory.

A more informative approach is to maintain an event table.

This distinction matters because a trading pair can disappear without the underlying token becoming worthless.

A token may continue trading elsewhere, migrate to a replacement contract, or remain withdrawable after exchange trading ends.

The event table should therefore preserve:

For strict point-in-time research, the announcement timestamp also matters.

A delisting decision may be announced before it becomes effective, and a strategy may respond to that information before the final trading date.

The next step is to compare two portfolio constructions.

The other uses only assets that remain active at the end of the sample.

This comparison illustrates how retrospective asset filtering changes the investment universe.

It does not yet calculate a valid strategy return.

For that, the researcher must also define the portfolio weights, rebalancing schedule, transaction costs, asset returns, and actual delisting treatment.

For a portfolio with asset weights (w_i) and asset returns (r_i), a simple one-period portfolio return is:

The weights should be calculated using only information available at the portfolio formation time.

The following example uses a simplified equal-weighted portfolio.

This example assumes that all four assets were held over the same measurement period, with an equal initial allocation and a final value of zero for ALT_B.

In an actual delisting study, that zero-value assumption must be supported by the documented economic outcome.

It is not appropriate to assign zero automatically to every delisted trading pair.

The model should instead distinguish the last executable exit opportunity, residual asset value, conversion arrangements, and transaction costs.

Point-in-time universe construction is only one part of avoiding historical information leakage.

For KAEL AI's quantitative research, these checks are particularly relevant when training models to rank digital assets or identify market opportunities.

A model cannot learn a reliable historical selection process if its training universe has already been filtered using future survival information.

Survivorship bias is not simply a missing-price problem.

It is a problem of reconstructing what the market actually contained at each historical decision point.

A more reliable backtesting framework needs historical asset directories, listing and delisting events, point-in-time eligibility rules, and documented terminal outcomes.

Python can help organize and test these components, but the quality of the results still depends on the completeness and accuracy of the underlying historical records.

A credible backtest must preserve the assets that disappeared, not just the assets that remain.

Ammann, M., Burdorf, T., Liebi, L., and Stöckl, S. (2022). Survivorship and Delisting Bias in Cryptocurrency Markets.

https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4287573

Web Scraping Data Completeness: Practical Solutions and Examples

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Data completeness is one of the most important challenges in web scraping. A scraper may execute successfully, return HTTP 200 responses, and still produce an incomplete dataset. For example, when scraping 1,000 product listings from an e-commerce website, your scraper might collect only 650 products because it failed to navigate all pagination pages.

In this article, we'll explore the common causes of incomplete datasets and practical solutions, including pagination handling, field validation, automated retries, website change detection, duplicate removal, and data completeness monitoring.

Here are practical engineering solutions to common data completeness challenges:

Pagination ensures your scraper captures records across all available pages, not just the first batch of results. Implement logic to follow Next Page links or page number parameters until no further records are available. Always verify that the final page has been reached to avoid missing data and producing incomplete datasets.

Problem: Your scraper collects data from the first page but misses subsequent records.

Solution: Implement pagination logic that continues until the last page is reached.

Engineering takeaway: Do not assume the first page contains all available records. Confirm pagination behavior using the website's actual structure.

Remark: This example assumes a site with ten pages. A production scraper should use a verified last page indicator or next-page link rather than a fixed limit.

Ensure every scraped record contains essential information, such as names, prices, URLs, or product IDs. Implement validation checks to identify missing or empty fields before storing records. This prevents incomplete data from entering your dataset and compromising downstream analysis.

Problem: Your scraper collects product records, but some contain missing names, prices, or URLs.

Remark: These records may be unsuitable for business analysis.

Solution: Validate required fields before storing the data.

This prevents records with missing essential information from silently entering your dataset.

In production, invalid records should also be logged for investigation rather than simply discarded.

Temporary network failures, server errors, and request timeouts can interrupt data collection. Use automated retry mechanisms with exponential backoff to recover from temporary failures and improve scraping consistency without overwhelming target websites.

Problem: Temporary network failures, server errors, or request timeouts interrupt data collection.

Solution: Configure automatic retries with longer waiting intervals after each failed request.

This allows the scraper to recover from certain temporary failures.

Respect the website's rate limits and retry instructions, especially for HTTP 429 responses.

Website layouts and HTML elements can change without notice, breaking existing scraping logic. Implement monitoring and validation checks to detect missing elements, unexpected response formats, or sudden drops in extracted records. This helps identify scraping failures early.

Problem: A website changes its HTML structure, causing the scraper to miss product information.

Solution: Monitor extraction results and alert when expected fields or record counts suddenly decline.

Repeated requests, overlapping pages, and crawling errors can introduce duplicate entries. Use unique identifiers, such as product IDs or canonical URLs, to identify and remove duplicates before storing the data. This improves dataset accuracy and prevents inflated record counts.

Problem: Products appear across multiple pages, causing duplicate entries.

Solution: Use a unique identifier such as a product ID or canonical URL.

Deduplication helps maintain accurate product counts and prevents misleading analytics.

Track expected versus collected records to measure how much required data your scraper captures. Set up automated alerts when extraction volumes drop below acceptable thresholds. Continuous monitoring helps identify gaps early and ensures datasets remain reliable for business analysis.

A production data pipeline should track completeness metrics.

In this scenario, the pipeline collected only 85% of the expected records. An automated alert could trigger an investigation before the incomplete dataset reaches a downstream analytics system.

Data completeness should be treated as a measurable engineering requirement, not an assumption.

Reliable web scraping pipelines combine pagination handling, field validation, retry mechanisms, structural change detection, deduplication, and monitoring.

For businesses, the goal is not simply to collect data. It is to ensure that the data collected is sufficiently complete, accurate, and consistent to support dependable decisions.

I help B2B SaaS, web data, and automation companies turn complex technical products into clear documentation, tutorials, and technical content that help developers understand their products and teams communicate their value. You can reach out to me via LinkedIn

Thirty days, zero customers: what the funnel data actually said

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I shipped a paid product on October 5th. Thirty days later: 3 indexed pages became 91, three months of search impressions went from 13 to 115, and the number of people who completed the form was zero.

This is what I learned reading the numbers carefully instead of optimistically.

Two things stand out. First, 123 visits in a month is nothing — that's not a funnel problem, that's a visibility problem. Second, both form starts happened on the same day, three days after launch, which is exactly when an initial wave of "people who saw the launch post" would arrive.

For the first two weeks I treated "0 conversions" as a conversion problem. I rewrote lock-screen copy, added a download button, added draft persistence, fixed a tax disclosure. All of that was real work and some of it was genuinely broken — but none of it could move a number that was gated on traffic I didn't have.

The lesson generalises: optimising conversion when N is small enough that Poisson noise dominates is not work, it's fidgeting. With 123 visits you cannot distinguish a 3% conversion rate from a 0% one.

Content depth. I compared my worst-ranking page to the top five competitors on the same query:

I was ranking ~82 with 17% of the median competitor's content. Expanded four pages to roughly double their length with real examples and FAQ blocks. Over the following days: indexed pages 62 → 91, impressions 50 → 115, and the specific keyword went from position 82.6 to 76.0.

That's a real movement and it's still page 8. Movement isn't the same as progress, but it's the first evidence the direction is right.

Backlinks. The site had two backlinks in total. Two. That's the actual reason nothing ranks — not the content, not the tech, not the copy. I spent a day testing ~25 directories and content platforms and found that:

Ended the day at ten backlinks, seven of them dofollow. Compared to two, that's the largest structural change of the month — and it will take 2–6 weeks to show up, if it does.

1. I had a bug that only affected paying customers, for weeks.

After checkout, the user was redirected to a page that verified the payment and then sent them back to an empty form — because the generated drafts were only in useState, and the redirect threw them away. Any customer would have had to refill nine questions and wait another minute.

I found it by reading my own code, not by using the product. The reason nobody reported it is that nobody bought.

An automated posting script printed posted nightly for eight days. Five of those days had posts. The account it was posting from had been silently placed in read-only mode, and my script treated "composer exists" as "post succeeded." Even in the days it worked, those posts got roughly 3 impressions each.

The distribution channel wasn't underperforming. It wasn't operating.

3. The marketing plan was written before the data.

There was a plan with phases: launch, then content, then paid acquisition. Written when the assumption was "we'll get some traffic from the launch and learn from it." The launch produced three visits. Everything downstream of that assumption was building on nothing.

Check whether your channels are real before optimising them. Not "is it running" but "did an external system acknowledge it." A post with no permalink didn't happen. A directory submission with no confirmation page didn't happen.

Look for bugs on the paid path specifically. Read the code from payment → unlock → delivery. Walk it as a customer. Nobody else is going to.

Compare against competitors on your worst-visible keyword. Not your best. Your best is ranking for a reason. Your worst-visible keyword is where the diagnosis is.

Count your backlinks. If it's under twenty and you're ranking on page 7–9 everywhere, stop looking at your own site. The problem is off-site.

There are two weeks left before a checkpoint I set for myself. The criteria: 30+ indexed pages (done, at 91), 5+ daily impressions on long-tail queries (currently ~1.3), at least one completed form (currently 0).

The honest read is that I'll miss two of three, and the reason won't be effort — it'll be that a thirty-day-old domain with ten backlinks doesn't rank, and no amount of polish changes that.

Which raises the question worth being explicit about: is the lesson "I executed badly" or "the timeline was wrong"?

I think it's mostly the latter, but I've been wrong about that before. If you've been through this, I'd genuinely like to know which one it was for you.

(The product is Speech Kobo. The one thing I built that's unambiguously useful regardless of how this goes: an open dataset.)

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

That was until now, when the official Secret Lair Twitter account teased the crossover, revealing it's set to drop in less than two weeks' time.

"The thumper is set and Shai-Hulud is on the move," Wizards of the Coast teased on social media, with a short video of a thumper pounding the sand, followed by the Secret Lair logo. "My Arrakis, My Superdrop goes on sale November 30. #DuneMovie"

This MTG Secret Lair tweet all but confirmed that everything in the recent leak was true. The name, "My Arrakis, My Superdrop," matches the one that was leaked, so we can probably assume everything else was correct.

It said there would be three individual drops based around Sandworms, artifacts, and enchantments, followed by a set of legendary creatures based on eight characters from the Dune movies. The set is reported to include reprints like Sandstorm Convergence and Darksteel Forge, but 11 of the 20 leaked cards are completely new to the Secret Lair drop.

Further details have been confirmed on Reddit, with one of the drops coming in Halo Foil. The commander cards will include some fan-favorite characters, including Paul Atreides, Baron Vladimir Harkonnen, and Chani. The third set, Spice Harvest, is the only one whose price has leaked so far, retailing for $ 59.99.

The Secret Lair Superdrop arrives just in time for the Dune: Part Three movie, set to release in theatres worldwide on December 18, the same day as Avengers: Doomsday. It will be the 13th Secret Lair Superdrop of the year, following the likes of The Hobbit, Lofi Girl, My Little Pony, and Star Trek.

We'll have more coverage of this drop coming soon to TheGamer, including a handy guide, so keep your eyes peeled.

Building Sites That Both Humans and AI Agents Can Read: A Practical Intro to AIO

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at zenn.dev

This article was originally published in Japanese on Zenn.

Hi, I'm Kohei Matsunobe, a second-year student at ZEN University studying Intelligent Informatics (online). I build AI agents and apps as personal projects, including an iOS app called Kamimegu and apps built with Expo / React Native.

The skills web engineers need from now on are not only "building sites that are easy for humans to read." Building sites that AI agents (such as GrokBot and Muse) can correctly recognize is becoming essential.

When you build AI agents, you notice that an agent is also a reader of the web. We are moving from a world where people click links in search results to one where agents collect and interpret information on the web and present answers or actions. In that world, the question for site builders is not only how a page looks, but whether a machine can read its structure without getting it wrong. I think of this as DX for AI: Data Experience.

I call this viewpoint AIO (AI Optimization). In this article I turn the four points from my post into minimal code:

A caveat up front: this is not a "do this and AI will definitely pick you up" guide. I haven't verified from primary sources how any specific AI agent parses a site, so I won't claim anything about that. What follows is based on official documentation and specs: how to make pages machine-readable, and how to signal your intent to crawlers.

Two pages can look identical, yet a page made only of divs doesn't tell a machine which part is the heading, the main content, or the navigation. A human can tell by looking. Something reading the HTML has far fewer clues beyond the tags.

This is also standard accessibility practice (screen readers). Here, "easy for humans to use" and "easy for machines to read" turn out to be the same work.

Side note: if your content only appears in the HTML after JavaScript runs, clients that don't execute JavaScript will see nothing. Whether a client runs JavaScript is up to that client, so it's safer to include important content in the initial HTML response (SSR / SSG). If you export Expo or React Native code to the web, keep this in mind for the pages you want to be read, such as a product page.

Semantic HTML describes a document's skeleton. Structured data states what a page is about (an article, an app, who wrote it) using the Schema.org vocabulary. Google recommends JSON-LD as the format (Google: Intro to structured data).

Google's Article documentation says there are no required properties; you add the recommended ones that apply (Google: Article structured data).

For an app's landing page, SoftwareApplication fits. These are dummy values:

You can browse the available types and properties at Schema.org/Article and Schema.org/SoftwareApplication. Adding structured data doesn't guarantee that any particular AI agent will use it. For Google Search features, the docs above are the primary source.

What matters here is that crawlers have different purposes. The vendors' own docs draw these distinctions:

(Sources at the end. Names and descriptions can change, so check each vendor's latest docs before configuring.)

With that in mind, a policy like "don't use my content for training, but I'm happy to appear in search" can be written like this:

If you want to allow everything, writing nothing (or Allow: / under User-agent: *) is enough. Which one is right depends on your site's policy.

llms.txt is a proposal by Jeremy Howard: a Markdown file summarizing a site for AI agents, placed at /llms.txt or another path.

Important: llms.txt is not a standard. It's a proposal. The spec page itself is worded "We propose...". Also, not every AI service reads it, and I haven't found a primary source confirming that any particular AI always does. I'd be wary of claims like "add it and your ranking or citations will go up" unless evidence is shown.

I still think it's worth trying because it's cheap. Per the spec (llmstxt.org), the file contains, in this order:

The same spec page also proposes serving clean Markdown versions of pages (page.html.md or page.md), without ads or navigation, for the links to point to.

On "access control": llms.txt is not control but guidance. It helps to separate roles: robots.txt for allow/deny signals, llms.txt for guidance.

This last point is unglamorous, but I think it's the most "engineer" part. These are my opinions.

I plan to review my own app landing pages and tech blog against this list.

My concern is this: a site that AI can't find, or can't understand the structure of, is becoming as good as nonexistent. But how each agent reads the web differs and will keep changing. That's why I think the durable move, for humans and AI alike, is not a quick trick for one specific AI, but writing meaning down properly, following standards.

Are you already starting to optimize for AI agents in your projects? I'd love to hear in the comments.

Google to keep Gemini chats for two months, up from 72 hours, even with history off

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google is extending how long it keeps temporary Gemini chats and conversations made with chat history turned off to around two months, saying the change aims to improve safety and security as AI systems become more capable.

The change, which will roll out globally in the Gemini app over the coming weeks, marks a significant increase from the previous 72-hour retention period mentioned in Google’s Gemini Privacy Hub, according to an archived version from November 12.

OpenAI, by comparison, says it may keep temporary chats in ChatGPT for up to 30 days, whereas Anthropic retains incognito chats in Claude for 30 days by default. However, companies may keep some data longer for legal or safety reasons, or to investigate policy violations.

Google did not answer questions about what prompted the change and whether it would affect how conversations are accessed or reviewed, instead pointing to a blog post that says chats are kept longer to respond to users, process feedback, and protect its services and users. Its updated Gemini Privacy Hub also cites potential system failures as a reason for retaining chats and says temporary conversations are not used to train AI models.

The longer retention period comes as Google introduces separate settings for Gemini’s chat history and AI training. Until now, both were tied to a single “Keep Activity” setting. Users will now be able to keep their conversations in their chat history without using them to train Google’s generative AI models.

Google says both new settings will be turned off by default for users who previously disabled Keep Activity. For those who had Keep Activity turned on, their chat history will remain enabled. They can, though, decide separately whether Google can use their conversations to train AI models.

Why Intel Stock Just Dropped

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sure, Nvidia sold 25% of its Intel stock -- but it kept 75%.

Pity Intel (INTC -2.76%) investors. Not only have they had to deal with the fallout of an Nvidia CEO scandal not of their making this month -- one that's contributed to an 18% drop in stock price in November alone.

Not only are they now struggling to parse a new SemiAnalysis report warning that capital spending on AI chips in 2027 could be 19% below previous estimates, 2028 spending might be 28% below, and 2029 spending could fall 41% below estimates.

Today, Intel investors are facing yet another negative catalyst for their stock. Nvidia (NVDA -1.47%), it seems, is selling down its stake in its semiconducting rival -- and it's costing Intel stock a 2.3% decline through 1:30 p.m. ET today.

Nvidia just confirmed in an SEC filing that it has sold 25% of its Intel stock position, reducing its stake from 214.8 million shares to 161.1 million. At the same time, as StreetInsider.com reports, Nvidia has increased its ownership in SpaceX (SPCX +2.69%) by 6%, to 130.5 million shares.

All of these numbers are as of Sept. 30, before the Jensen Huang scandal broke.

Nvidia's moves proved prescient. Although SpaceX stock has fallen slightly since the report date (down about 6%), Intel shares are down much, much more -- roughly 55%. So Nvidia kept a lot of its Intel profits by cashing out before Intel crashed (a crash that Nvidia's own CEO scandal helped cause).

The question for Intel investors now is: should you follow Nvidia's example and sell out of Intel, too? Before you do that, recall that if Nvidia sold 25% of its Intel shares in September, then it also kept 75%.

So Nvidia hasn't abandoned all hope in Intel yet. Maybe you shouldn't either?

ULA lost a Vulcan Centaur rocket carrying 40 Amazon satellites 10 minutes after launch — but the failure began just after liftoff

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"Early indications are that just after liftoff the launch vehicle experienced low booster performance."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The failure of a Vulcan Centaur rocket carrying a full load of internet satellites for Amazon was caused by an anomaly in its first-stage booster, rocket maker United Launch Alliance said today (Nov. 17).

That failure — which led to the loss of 40 Amazon Leo internet satellites — was recognized about 10 minutes after liftoff, but the problems began much earlier than that, ULA said in a statement.

"While we appear to have lost telemetry and the mission at approximately 10 minutes into flight, early indications are that just after liftoff the launch vehicle experienced low booster performance, resulting in stage separation occurring when the vehicle was low in altitude and below minimal velocity to reach orbit," ULA wrote in today's update.

The two-stage Vulcan Centaur is the successor to ULA's workhorse Atlas V rocket, which will retire after just six more flights. Vulcan debuted in January 2024 and now has a total of five launches under its belt.

The new rocket has experienced problems on three of those missions. On both its second and fourth flights — which launched in October 2024 and February of this year, respectively — the nozzle of one of Vulcan's strap-on solid rocket boosters (SRBs) fell off about 30 seconds after liftoff.

On both occasions, the rocket overcame the problem and successfully delivered its payloads to orbit. However, after the second occurrence, the U.S. Space Force paused national-security launches with Vulcan until ULA addressed the SRB nozzle issue. (The February 2026 flight was a Space Force mission.)

That work led to "design and process improvements to the nozzle," ULA said in an Oct. 28 update. And those tweaks apparently worked.

"All indications are that the solid rocket booster performance was nominal, following nozzle improvements implemented for this launch," the company wrote in today's update.

Vulcan's Centaur V upper stage, which features two RL-10C engines, did its job as well "but was unable to recover from the [booster] performance loss, resulting in loss of mission," ULA added.

Vulcan's first-stage booster is powered by two BE-4 engines, which are built by Amazon founder Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin. Today's update did not identify the exact problem with the booster; doing so will require more time, as the investigation has just begun.

If that investigation finds a serious issue with the BE-4, there could be broad ripple effects across the American space program. After all, that engine also powers the first stage of Blue Origin's New Glenn rocket, which is a key piece of hardware for NASA's Artemis program of moon exploration. (And New Glenn is currently recovering from a failure of its own — a massive explosion on the test stand that occurred on May 28.)

NASA plans to integrate the Centaur V upper stage into its Space Launch System moon rocket, the vehicle that launches Artemis astronauts to space. So the fact that the Centaur performed nominally on Sunday is a bit of good news for the space agency.

Vulcan's grounding is a big deal, no matter what the anomaly investigation ends up finding. The U.S. doesn't have a surfeit of powerful rockets, after all, and two of them — Vulcan and New Glenn — are out of commission at the moment.

There's considerable pressure on both Vulcan and New Glenn to bounce back strong, for the nation faces an impending launch crunch in the next few years. SpaceX plans to retire its Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy rockets and shift its launch load over to the fully reusable Starship megarocket. SpaceX is expected to prioritize its own needs in Starship's early days, using the vehicle primarily to build out its next-gen Starlink broadband constellation and Starmind AI data center network.

The Vulcan failure also impacts Amazon — specifically, the company's plan to build out Amazon Leo, which will compete with SpaceX's Starlink. Amazon has booked about 100 launches on a variety of rockets to build its constellation, which will eventually consist of more than 3,200 satellites. Thirty-eight of those bookings are for Vulcan; Sunday's launch was the first of those.

The other rockets Amazon has booked are the Atlas V, New Glenn, Falcon 9 and Arianespace's Ariane 6. About 400 operational Amazon Leo satellites have reached orbit to date across 14 launches (not including Sunday's failure).

He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. It was Odegaard’s 72nd-minute strike that proved the difference before the international break, when Mikel Arteta’s men were made to work for their victory at home to newly promoted Hull City.

The Norway international will no doubt be desperate to pick up where he left off when the north London giants return to top-flight against at Newcastle on Saturday evening.

The femtoAI fully integrated AI silicon and compression platform uniquely enables Legato SpeechSenseâ¢ AI

SAN BRUNO, Calif., Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- femtoAI, a pioneer of a fully integrated AI silicon and compression platform, proudly congratulates Legato Hearing on the success of Legato Frames, award-winning AI hearing glasses that deliver its patented Legato SpeechSenseâ¢ AI with femtoAI.The femtoAI SPU-001 AI processor and cutting-edge AI software, along with its open developer support and collaborative tuning from the femtoAI team, empowered Legato Frames from concept to a shipping product.

Legato Frames are premium eyeglasses with hearing technology built entirely into the frame, so nothing goes in the wearer's ears. Legato SpeechSenseâ¢ AI is what makes that possible: an on-device AI algorithm stack that isolates speech from background noise in real time and amplifies only the speech using less power to enable a longer daily battery life .SpeechSenseâ¢ pairs a custom implementation of femtoAI algorithm with Legato's own proprietary signal processing. The femtoAI platform, including the SPU-001 chip, contributes configurable blocks for feedback cancellation, speech enhancement and noise reduction, wide dynamic range compression, and frequency shifting. Together, the solution delivers best-in-class hearing enhancement in a 10X smaller footprint and at 10X lower power, without sacrificing accuracy.

HearAdvisor, a lab that benchmarks the world's leading hearing aids, scored them 4.6 out of 5 on its SoundScore scale, placing them among top prescription hearing aids that cost thousands of dollars on average. AirJoule Technologies: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pilot results will decide which of the two new platforms earns the capital, and the article leaves that question open for now.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, AirJoule Technologies (AIRJ -14.46%) announced the acquisition of data center infrastructure provider BitSink alongside a refined platform roadmap. The transaction gives the company commercial revenue and access to fast-growing artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure markets. Three developments from the call highlight how management plans to unlock long-term shareholder value.

AirJoule acquired BitSink for a total of $27 million in upfront consideration. The transaction adds manufacturing capacity and $15 million in signed purchase orders for modular data center blocks. It gives the business a commercial footprint while creating a path to co-locate water generation units at customer sites with cooling demand.

Management split its core technology into two distinct offerings based on initial pilot results. Prime serves large-scale water generation where supply is scarce, while Core DH (a dedicated dehumidification platform) targets more than 40% energy savings compared with desiccant systems because it doesn't rely on traditional heating cycles.

The company delivered its first Prime water generation system to Data4 in France to demonstrate the capture of waste heat from operational servers. That deployment showcases the technology to hub members as European regulations begin to require facility waste-heat utilization.

Commercial momentum accelerates as real-world deployment data guides capital allocation across four distinct market verticals. A joint venture (JV, a business arrangement where two companies pool resources) with GE Vernova and a modular factory model provide structural production advantages as management monitors manufacturing execution.

Standardized block manufacturing and strategic customer pilots position the platform to address mounting power, water, and cooling constraints in digital infrastructure.

3 AI Infrastructure Stocks to Buy Hand Over Fist if the Market Pulls Back

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investors have been selling AI stocks as if the build-out were about to stop. The companies wiring, connecting, and building the data centers just said the opposite. Here are three I'd keep buying on any dip.

Wall Street spent the past few weeks selling artificial intelligence (AI) stocks as if the data center boom was about to stall. The companies actually doing the building keep saying the opposite.

The Nasdaq Composite fell into a correction on Nov. 5 after the midterm results raised fears of new limits on data centers. By Nov. 6, the PHLX Semiconductor index sat roughly 40% below its June high. Then Nvidia (NVDA -1.50%) CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Nov. 12. The Nasdaq still gained 2.3% last week, closing Nov. 13 at 25,136.27.

That bounce could easily fade. If it does, these are the three AI stocks I'd be buying with both hands, because none of them depends on which chip wins.

Eaton (ETN -0.40%) makes the electrical gear that takes power from the grid and safely feeds it to racks of servers: switchgear, breakers, and power distribution systems. Its data center revenue rose about 65% last quarter, and data center orders more than doubled over the past 12 months.

What I like most is the profit side. Eaton has been racing to add factory space, and 18 of its 25 capacity projects are now complete. Operating margin in its Americas electrical business, the share of each sales dollar left as profit, jumped 2.3 percentage points in a single quarter to 29.8%.

Eaton is also reaching closer to the chip itself. Its Boyd Thermal unit, which makes liquid cooling systems for AI servers, roughly tripled its backlog from a year ago. Management raised its full-year forecast for the third time this year, to $13.70 to $13.80 in adjusted earnings per share. At $352.97, the stock trades about 26% below its 52-week high. I'd buy it now and buy more on any dip, because the expensive part of the build-out is behind it and the payoff is just starting.

Corning (GLW +1.21%) is best known for the glass on your phone, but these days its biggest business is optical fiber, the hair-thin glass strands that carry data between servers. Optical sales jumped 44% to $2.38 billion last quarter, and sales of its products for generative AI more than doubled.

CEO Wendell Weeks said that AI growth is outpacing growth in graphics chip shipments. In plain English, every new AI server needs more of Corning's fiber than the one before it.

Corning also signed four long-term, multibillion-dollar customer deals last quarter, including with Verizon Communications and AT&T, and says those agreements share the cost of building new capacity. That's a major reason Corning generated $2.27 billion in adjusted free cash flow in the first nine months of 2026 while continuing to expand.

Even so, the stock closed at $114.08 on Nov. 13, about 58% below its 52-week high. Management expects fourth-quarter core earnings per share of $0.96 to $1, up about 36% from last year. For a company aiming to double its annual sales pace from $20 billion to $40 billion by the end of 2030, that's a price-to-earnings ratio I'm happy to pay.

Someone has to physically install all of that equipment, and Emcor (EME +0.56%) is a leader in electrical and mechanical construction, the wiring, piping, and cooling inside big buildings. Its remaining performance obligations, meaning signed work it hasn't finished yet, hit a record $18.57 billion, up 47% from a year ago. The biggest jump came from data center work, which requires heavy electrical systems and increasingly complex cooling systems.

The fun detail is cooling. AI chips run so hot that the piping and cooling work inside these buildings keeps growing. In Emcor's mechanical business, revenue from network and communications jobs, which are mostly data centers, reached $985.2 million last quarter, up from $432.1 million a year earlier.

Emcor raised its 2026 forecast to $34.50 to $35.50 in adjusted earnings per share and added $500 million to its stock buyback program. On Nov. 13, it agreed to buy P1 Construction, a Kansas contractor that builds hyperscale data centers and expects up to $650 million in revenue this year, paying with cash on hand. It can afford to, with just $6 million in total debt at the end of September.

Shares closed at $659.36, about 31% below their 52-week high. That's a bargain for a company whose signed work grew 47% while sales grew 29%, and it's the one I'd reach for first.

The market is pricing these three stocks as if the build-out ends in a few months. Their order books, signed contracts, and new factories say they'll be busy well into 2027, and that gap is exactly what I'd want to buy into if the market drops again.

How Does The REO Runabout Stack Up Against Slate?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

Like the idea of a Slate but don't want an EV? Well, an upstart from Dallas, Texas has just the thing for you. That is, if it ever becomes a reality. Startup company REO recently unveiled renderings of its new truck, dubbed the Runabout. It's a similar idea to Slate, with just one basic frame, three different body variants to choose from, no frills, a bunch of customization, and a sub-$30,000 price tag. The main difference is that that Runabout uses a four-cylinder gas engine, rather than electric motors. So how does it compare to Slate?

First, let's look at price. The most basic Slate you can get—cheekily called "The Blank Slate"—costs $24,950, and it's a two-door pickup with nothing more than some seats, a steering wheel, a battery, and a single rear electric motor to get you around. There's no radio, power windows, or screens to speak of.

The cheapest REO you can get will be the Runabout T4X, which costs $21,500*. Note the asterisk, as REO says the current listed price is an estimate, and the company has no factory or clear plans for getting it to market. For that shockingly low price, it's estimated that you'll get a four-cylinder engine, a choice of either a six-speed manual or automatic transmission, and mechanical four-wheel drive. It's a two-door, two-seat pickup with drop-down sides for the bed, like a Kei truck.

What four-cylinder engine will the Runabout have and which manufacturer will supply it? REO isn't saying. There are far more questions than answers so far. REO does say that it has a "targeted powertrain life of 500,000 miles." To quote myself reading that in the press release: "lol."

While Slate uses a hybrid body-on-frame style chassis, the REO has an old-school ladder frame, like a classic pickup. It's similar to slate in that it offers modular body styles, but with key differences. There's a two-door pickup and an SUV version, like Slate, but there's also a four-door pickup, which Slate doesn't have. REO claims its truck has solid rear axles, too, to give it more off-road credibility.

The design isn't bad, even if it looks like a Grand Theft Auto version of an Australian-market Land Cruiser, but that's to be expected. REO hired longtime automotive designer Frank Stephenson, whose previous work includes the first BMW-owned Mini Cooper, the Maserati MC12, and the McLaren P1. Overall, it looks handsome enough and utilitarian, even if all the photos are just renders at this point.

Inside, REO's truck is said to be pretty barebones (it hasn't been revealed yet), but not as barebones as Slate's. There's a digital screen for gauges and one for navigation, but everything else has a physical control. You're probably wondering if it has power or hand-crank windows. REO used its press release to throw a little shade: " Each model will feature power windows for convenience: hand-cranks are best left to others."

The entire idea behind REO is to offer the sort of basic pickups customers loved in the '80s and '90s, trucks that were simple utilitarian workhorses and nothing else. It's an admirable goal, especially given how expensive pickups have become. But do customers want to go backward in powertrain technology, especially with affordable mainstream electric trucks like the Ford Fathom coming to market soon?

So for less money, you can get a potentially more capable truck, in a similar configuration as the Slate, with a bit of extra interior comfort. But you lose the electric powertrain, which reduces maintenance and fuel costs. Instead, you'll have to make due with a generic four-cylinder of some sort. There is one big "if," though. You can only buy one if REO ever actually makes it. Sure, having a design by Frank Stephenson helps it feel more real, but just because they found the money to pay him to design something doesn't mean they'll actually get around to building it.

Slate is already here, it's ready to launch production vehicles soon. We've seen them in person, touched them, and tested them. Until REO can actually muster a viable product, it should probably avoid throwing anymore shade in its press releases.

Worried About the Tech Sector? Buy This Vanguard ETF for Long-Term Safety and Dividends

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investing in high-quality utility stocks can be a great move for risk-averse investors right now.

Now can be a risky time to invest in tech or even have exposure to it. Between rising interest rates and a Chinese spying scandal involving Nvidia, one of the most popular artificial intelligence companies in the world, there's plenty of bearishness in the markets these days.

Investors can seek safety, however, by pivoting to high-quality Vanguard funds, which can provide not only long-term stability and safety, but also a great deal of recurring income.

A top fund that may be ideal for risk-averse investors who don't want to worry about tech's troubles these days is the Vanguard Utilities ETF (VPU -0.89%).

Utility companies generate recurring, predictable earnings numbers. There may be volatility due to weather, but by and large, many of them are fairly safe and stable businesses to invest in. Given that they provide customers with essential services, they can be precisely the type of safe-haven assets worth investing in right now.

Through the Vanguard Utilities ETF, investors don't need to worry about picking the best ones, either. This can be ideal for investors who aren't familiar with utility stocks but who do want exposure to the sector.

And with 68 stocks in its portfolio, this Vanguard fund offers some excellent diversification. Its top three holdings include leading utility providers in the country -- NextEra Energy, Southern Company, and Duke Energy. Together, they make up 25% of the portfolio. The vast majority of the stocks in the fund, however, only make up 2%. Focusing on the big names in the sector is an excellent way to offer the best of both worlds: exposure to the leading companies while still offering a diversified mix of holdings.

This Vanguard ETF has generated modest returns of 2% this year, but when including its dividend, its total returns are up around 5%. With a yield of nearly 3%, the fund can generate substantially more income for investors than simply tracking the S&P 500 index, which currently averages just 1.1%.

With an extremely low expense ratio of 0.09%, an above-average payout, and top utility stocks in its portfolio, the Vanguard fund can be a solid investment for virtually any type of investor. And it can also be an ideal one to hang on to for the long haul.

Disney Touts Its Streaming Success In Latest Earnings

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After a lot of internal upheaval and consolidation, the House of Mouse finds itself in a pretty good place going into its fiscal 2027 year.

So says new CEO Josh D’Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston during an earnings call on Tuesday. They told investors that Disney exceeded its guidance for the previous quarter and is on track to exceed its three-year adjusted earnings-per-share growth guidance going into the new year.

For the full year, revenue not only increased 8% but broke the $100 billion mark, from $94.billion in the fiscal year 2025 to $102.3 billion in 2026.

Meanwhile, for Disney’s Q4 (the company starts its fiscal year a quarter earlier), revenue increased 16% to $25.9 billion for last quarter, compared to $22.5 the year before.

Within entertainment, advertising grew 10% to $1.7 billion. And SVOD revenue increased 19% to $6.1 billion, with 22% subscription revenue growth.

In TV, D’Amaro highlighted the critical buzz around Hulu’s crime thriller “Furious,” high ratings for the new season of “Dancing with the Stars” and excitement over “The Remarried Empress,” a South Korean romantasy that debuted as the biggest premiere for a local original across Disney+ and Hulu globally. The latter is indicative of Disney’s plan to invest more in international content, a strategy that’s been working wonders for competitors like Netflix.

Sports are also a big deal, of course. Subscription and affiliate fee revenue in the sports segment increased 18% in the quarter to $3.4 billion, with advertising revenue growth of 20%.

Disney will continue to grow its sports footprint next year with expanded college football rights, increased “Monday Night Football” ratings and ESPN’s first ever Super Bowl telecast. (Well, technically it’s ABC’s turn to host the Big Game, but it’ll also be cross-broadcast on other Disney channels.)

Where Disney’s overall streaming strategy is concerned, D’Amaro told investors that there will be a larger focus on “connecting services” – which includes other non-entertainment sides of the business like parks, cruise ships and merchandise.

More practically, though, “connecting services” means finally unifying Disney+ and Hulu into one app, which D’Amaro said would start in December. It also means adding more sports content to Disney+, and introducing creator content from TikTok into the Disney+ mobile app next month.  The move might strike some as strange, but given that ESPN’s short-form Verts product broke records in September and October, maybe there’s something to it.

At the same time, Disney is still exploring the idea of a free streaming product, which leadership first discussed during last quarter’s earnings reports.

Despite – or maybe because of – all the streaming success, Disney announced it will stop reporting its SVOD operating income beginning next quarter. At the same time, the company will also provide additional disclosures related to revenue within the entertainment segment.

SVOD revenues will still be reported, Johnston clarified to investors but so will content and IP revenues. Revenue for linear networks will be combined with the virtual MVPD business.

The goal, said Johnston, is to provide investors with “additional information and a better lens into our content monetization,” while allowing Disney’s team more flexibility to “invest appropriately” in growing SVOD.

In other words, Disney’s likely planning to spend a lot more money on its SVOD business – and maybe it doesn’t want investors to worry too much about how the sausage is getting made.

Disney’s remarkable growth this past quarter isn’t surprising, given how much the company has restructured since D’Amaro took on the CEO role in March. At least 1000 members of Disney’s workforce have been laid off since then, with more job cuts and consolidation reportedly on the way.

All that streamlining is expected to translate into $3 billion in run rate savings by 2029, according to D’Amaro.

To highlight the ways Disney is prioritizing efficiency and consolidation, D’Amaro also shouted out the company’s first ever chief technology officer, Karandeep Anand, who came from the (now bankrupt) chatbot startup Character.ai.

Not surprisingly, Disney plans to continue investing in AI tools and technology across its entire business, even parks. Although D’Amaro didn’t bring up the company’s recent purchase of Character.ai’s assets, he did hype up how the tech is being used in the streaming side of the business, both within Disney+’s content recommendation engine and in the advertising products Disney makes available to its marketing clients.

In addition to Disney’s AI creative studio that uses existing brand material to develop CTV-ready ads, the company also launched an agentic ad campaign tool in August. Both were previewed at its tech and data showcase at CES earlier this year.

Now, almost half of all new ad campaigns are flowing through the agentic tool, according to Disney’s ad sales team.

After selling ads at The Wall Street Journal and WebMD and rising to CRO at The Trade Desk, Jed Dederick is now the first-ever chief revenue officer of creator startup ShopMy.

DecryptAds’ goal, Garcia said, is to give researchers, ad agencies, tech companies and regulators the evidence they need to fix security gaps in the programmatic supply chain.

The mobile growth marketing platform Liftoff reported its second-ever quarterly earnings last week. Shares were up by almost 25% after the company exceeded its revenue target and projected an optimistic profit margin outlook moving forward.

CTV sellers are promising measurable outcomes – even guaranteed store visits – but winning advertiser trust takes more than just measurement.

For the first time, a new feature in Google Ads breaks down the dollar amount a campaign saved from IVT blocking and exactly how much Google is crediting the campaign for bots caught post-billing.

Tim and Chris Vanderhook explain why Viant bought Waymark and how it fits into their ambitious plans for CTV.
