Clouds block satellite cameras, so an AI learned how to redraw the ground beneath them

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GeoCore-9B learned how to recreate the ground that is hidden beneath cloud cover in satellite photos.

A satellite picture of a flooded town is only worth something if the camera can see the town. On a cloudy day it can’t, and the picture shows cloud where the streets should be. A new AI model fills those streets back in.

It was built to know where on Earth it is looking and how much ground each pixel covers.

Jeonghyeok Do and Munchurl Kim of KAIST in South Korea built the model, called GeoCore-9B. Their paper is a preprint that has been accepted for presentation at NeurIPS 2026, an artificial intelligence conference. It has not yet been published in a journal.

The ground the model draws under a cloud is a reconstruction, not a photograph. Do and Kim make that point themselves, and they warn that realistic fake satellite imagery could be used to spread disinformation about disasters or geopolitical events.

Ordinary satellite pictures work like a camera. They record sunlight bouncing off the ground, so thick cloud or nighttime leaves them with nothing to record.

That matters most during floods and wildfires, when the weather is often bad and the ground is exactly what people need to see.

A radar satellite sends radio waves down and measures the echoes that come back. The method is called synthetic aperture radar, and it works at night and through cloud.

But its images don’t look like photographs, because brightness and texture in them mean different things. Do and Kim wanted one model for both problems.

Earlier attempts started with an AI built to generate ordinary photographs and then taught it about satellites. Do and Kim wrote that this builds in biases.

Ordinary photos are taken from eye level with a subject in the middle, while a satellite looks straight down at a scene with no center.

So they trained their AI model from scratch on satellite views alone, adjusting about 9 billion internal settings, known as parameters, as it learned.

The training set was 10 million satellite images, each paired with a sentence describing it. The note for a port mentioned ships and warehouses; the note for farmland mentioned a road between fields.

Each image also carried its latitude, longitude, and how much ground one pixel covers. A building looks very different when each pixel covers about 3 feet (1 meter) than when each covers about 105 feet (32 meters).

Without that number, a small building and a large industrial plant can have the same shape.

A model this large, trained from nothing, learns slowly and tends to produce scenes with no coherent layout. So Do and Kim brought in a second AI, already trained to recognize satellite imagery, as a teacher.

During training, the new model was scored on how well its internal picture of a scene agreed with the teacher’s picture of how roads connect and where field boundaries lie.

The teacher is switched off once training ends, so it adds no work when the model is in use. Without it, the team found, the model’s images had fragmented textures and distorted structures. With it, every score they tracked improved.

The team also tested what the model had absorbed about geography. Given only a latitude and longitude, with no text at all, it drew dense skyscrapers for the coordinates of New York City and ice fields for Antarctica. It drew sand dunes for the Sahara and forest for the Amazon.

The team checked 500 of these coordinate-only images generated by the model against all 10.5 million real world photos that were used as training images.

They found none that closely matched the real-world satellite images, suggesting that the AI was not simply copying existing photographs. However, they wrote that the test can’t rule out every form of memorization used by the AI model.

To put the model to work, Do and Kim froze it and trained a small set of extra settings for each task. This was far cheaper than retraining it.

On a public test set of cloudy and clear image pairs, GeoCore-9B outscored every program built specifically for cloud removal on two of three standard measures. Those were cover brightness and color accuracy, and how closely the structure matches the clear image.

On the third, how natural the image looks, it scored about the same as the best specialist. The roads and field boundaries in its reconstructions stayed where the clear images put them.

On a second public dataset, the model turned radar images into photo-like images. It scored best on the two measures of realism and fine detail, and it roughly matched the best specialist on structural similarity.

“By training the AI on where each satellite image was taken and the actual size of the terrain it shows, we made it possible to use a single model for multiple tasks such as cloud removal and image conversion,” said Kim, a professor in KAIST’s School of Electrical Engineering.

He added that he expects the work to make weather-limited satellite data more usable for assessing disasters and monitoring changes in forests and cities.

The model only produces ordinary color images. It can read a radar image but can’t generate one. In addition, it doesn’t handle the multispectral images, with light bands beyond visible color, that some Earth-observation satellites record.

One piece, the component that compresses each image before the model works on it, came from ordinary photography. On the cloud-removal images, it kept the main features but lost some faint local detail.

By their own account, the experiments can’t say how much of the gain came from the model’s size, its training data, or the teacher.

So far each task has been tested on a single public dataset. How the model performs on other satellites and regions is unknown, and that is the test Do and Kim plan next.

Do and Kim also want the model to predict changes on the ground: flooding, building damage, wildfire spread, and urban growth. The team plans to explore surveillance and reconnaissance as well.

The model’s code and weights are online, so other groups can run those tests.

The full study has been posted as a preprint on arXiv and accepted for presentation at NeurIPS 2026. It has not been published in a journal.

Voyager 1 reaches 1 light-day from Earth

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 5:16 a.m. EST on Wednesday, Nov. 18, Voyager 1 will become the first spacecraft to reach a distance of 1 light-day from Earth. A command radioed from home will need 24 hours to arrive, and a reply another 24 to come back. NASA is asking the public to celebrate by sending the spacecraft a signal on the evening of Tuesday, Nov. 17.

NASA launched the twin Voyager spacecraft in 1977 to tour the giant planets: Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. Voyager 1 flew past Jupiter and Saturn, while Voyager 2 went on to Uranus and Neptune and is still the only spacecraft to visit either one. Both then kept going. They’re now the only probes ever to study interstellar space directly, the region beyond the Sun’s magnetic field.

Each spacecraft runs on a radioisotope thermoelectric generator, the same kind of power source that drives NASA’s Curiosity and Perseverance rovers on Mars. Plutonium-238, the fuel source, gives off heat as it naturally decays, and the generators turn that heat into electricity. But because the output fades by about 4 watts every year, engineers have spent years shutting off components and instruments to stretch the spacecraft’s lives. This year, a risky maneuver nicknamed the “Big Bang” turned off several devices in one coordinated move to buy the spacecraft and their remaining instruments several more years of operation. Today, Voyager 1 only runs two of its original 10 instruments, and Voyager 2 runs three.

Voyager 1 is closing in on 1 light-day from Earth (about 16.1 billion miles [25.9 billion kilometers]) — farther than any spacecraft has ever gone. Its twin is about 2 billion miles (3.2 billion km) closer to Earth, near enough that a signal takes about 20 hours to reach Voyager 2.

Voyager 1 lifted off Sept. 5, 1977, two weeks after Voyager 2 but on a shorter trajectory as it was traveling to Jupiter. After visiting Saturn in 1980, it drifted away from the solar system’s planets for good. A decade later, it captured the famous photograph of Earth known as the Pale Blue Dot. In August 2012, it became the first spacecraft to enter interstellar space, and Voyager 2 followed in 2018. Voyager 2 won’t reach the light-day mark until November 2035. You can see both missions’ exact locations on NASA’s mission status page.

A light-day is a distance measuring how far light travels in 24 hours. At about 186,000 miles per second (299,792 km per second), that adds up to roughly 16.1 billion miles (25.9 billion km). For scale, the Moon is just 1.3 light-seconds away, the Sun about 8.3 light-minutes, and Neptune a bit over 4 light-hours. Proxima Centauri, the nearest star beyond the Sun, lies more than 1,500 light-days away.

At a speed of nearly 38,000 mph (61,000 km/h), Voyager 1 is anything but slow. Still, it needed more than 49 years to cover the distance light crosses in a single day.

Astronomy usually asks readers to dim their lights. But tomorrow night is an exception: To celebrate Voyager 1’s accomplishment, NASA invites everyone to flash Voyager’s call sign, V-G-R, skyward in Morse code on Tuesday evening. Use NASA’s automated flasher on your smartphone or tablet. Or grab a plain flashlight and do it manually: V: dot-dot-dot-dash; G: dash-dash-dot; R: dot-dash-dot. Dots are quick blinks of the light, and dashes are long flashes of about a second. Pause between letters.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

How Each Music Listening Platform Is Treating AI Music (Updating)

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From Bandcamp's outright ban of AI music to Spotify's "AI Personas" approach, here's how all the music platforms are dealing with the flood of AI-generated content.

One of the most pressing questions in the music industry right now is how streaming services should be treating the deluge of AI-generated music on their platforms. Should AI music be demonetized, or even removed from platforms? Should it be labeled or flagged so that users know what they are listening to? Or, for those optimistic about AI’s creative possibilities, should it all just be left alone?

It’s hard to know the exact scale of AI music on streaming services today, but what is clear is that there’s a lot of it — and the amount of it is growing fast. Deezer reports the count of daily AI track uploads at over 50% at “peak level” in June 2026, and Apple Music told Billboard in April that “more than a third” of monthly uploads were 100% AI-generated. Qobuz also noted in January that 34% of new uploads each day are AI-generated. These metrics, however, may still represent an undercount of all the material that uses AI in some part of the creative process, given partially AI generated music is much more challenging to detect. Plus, it’s impossible to determine when AI is used to write lyrics or melodies that are then recorded by human musicians, unless a user offered up their prompting history.

When considering the challenges of actually implementing regulations on streaming services, the question of what to do about AI music becomes incredibly complex. There’s a risk of false positives with AI detection tools, but requiring self-disclosure from rights holders will likely lead to outright deception at worst, or innocent omission at best, given how prevalent AI elements can be at any point in the recording process.

Still, lines are being drawn, and until some sort of consensus emerges, those lines are different at different digital service providers. To learn more about what each music listening platform is doing about AI-generated music, Billboard has compiled an updating list of each services’ policies as they articulate them.

SoundCloud bans all AI-generated content on its platform that impersonates human artists or otherwise clones their voices, and it requires that AI-generated or manipulated content complies with the same platform requirements as any other music uploaded to SoundCloud. The platform itself has said it does not use any music content to train generative AI models and would not do so any time in the future without prior consent.

In an open letter in November 2026, SoundCloud CEO Eliah Seton wrote that the company will also be adding protections against users who use AI to spam the platform with slop, and it will beef up its defenses against streaming manipulation, a common byproduct of AI. Those who violate its policies will be blocked from the platform.

SoundCloud, however, has no issue with AI music on its platform in general. Unlike other listening platforms, SoundCloud has leaned into partnering with compliant, licensed AI music companies, like ElevenLabs and Moises, to provide a direct pipeline for AI users to share their work online.

Spotify is attempting to tackle the negative uses of AI music, while leaving the door open for users to enjoy the songs if they want to. Last year, the service announced policies against spam, impersonation and streaming fraud — all of which are not issues specific to AI, necessarily, but that can be exacerbated by the technology. In April 2026, Spotify provided a way for creators using AI to disclose exactly how they did it in the credits, including whether AI was used in just one guitar lick or in the entire production.

The service also announced verification badges for human creators and is rolling out a service called Artist Profile Protection which allows participants to essentially lock their artist pages from getting random uploads added to them, a persistent issue on Spotify and other streaming services.

The company’s boldest step yet came in August when the Swedish streamer announced the creation of a new label that will be applied to AI-generated artists and begin rolling out in mid-September. Music from artists bearing the label, called “AI Persona,” will not be included in personalized recommendations on the platform.

Apple Music is working towards visible labels for AI-generated music on the platform. The system, dubbed Made With AI, was announced in August and is set to roll out by the end of 2026. The platform noted that any song that is tagged as being “materially generated using AI” by content providers, like record labels and distributors, will receive these labels.

The move builds on Apple Music’s March announcement, requiring content providers to disclose and tag where AI is being used in the creative process, whether that’s in an upload’s artwork, recording, composition or music video. The system relies on disclosure, rather than an automated AI detection and flagging system, even though Apple Music head Oliver Schusser has told Billboard the company has a detector the team uses internally to monitor the rise of AI content.

YouTube requires that anyone uploading generative AI content, including music, discloses when AI has been used. Then, the platform adds a label to that content for viewer transparency. Beyond that, YouTube has not formed other policies around AI music specifically. (Google, YouTube’s parent company, has also been pursuing an AI music-making model of its own, Lyria 3, after acquiring ProducerAI earlier this year.)

Amazon Music has systems in place to prevent impersonation, deception and spam, all of which are commonly found in AI music uploads, but beyond that the company does not have any policies related to AI music. In a statement, Amazon said, “The broader music community is defining what labeling standards should look like, and we’re part of those conversations. As those discussions continue, we’ll continue to explore ways to help customers make informed choices about what they’re listening to.”

In June, Tidal, which had previously stayed silent on the issue of AI-generated music, came out with some of the strictest rules against the content type in the industry. This included the announcement that Tidal will not monetize AI-generated content and is making distributors responsible for identifying AI-generated music before uploading it. It will also try to identify AI content itself and will administer labels for AI music, which began in mid-July, so that users know the origins of what music they are hearing. This labeling system will start with fully AI generated music before being expanded to identify and label “substantially AI-generated” music as technology improves and “AI-detection methods become more reliable.”

“The responsibility to identify and tag AI-generated content should not rest with Tidal alone,” the company said in a press release. “We expect — and will begin to enforce — that content distributors identify AI-generated content before it reaches our platform.”

In January, Qobuz announced that it is “taking a human-first stand on AI-generated music,” and vowed that its editorial curation — which includes playlists, articles and reviews created by human experts — would remain “100% human,” and that it would work to ensure its “Discover” page “filter[s] out AI-generated tracks.” The service also said it would identify fraudulent streams and exclude them from the royalty pool and that it was “in the process of developing detection and monitoring systems to identify AI-generated content and fraudulent streaming patterns.”

Additionally, Qobuz now has contractual clauses prohibiting delivery of 100% AI-generated music, and it will integrate “dedicated DDEX fields for artists and labels to report AI-generated content.”

Deezer has been the most outspoken of the streaming services about AI music thus far. In January 2025, the French service began reporting the number of AI songs it was detecting on its service. Back then, it was reported that 10% of daily uploads were fully AI-generated, according to their in-house detector. Now, as of July, it was reported as more than 50% at “peak level in June 2026.”

The company has announced a number of policies designed to combat the negative uses of AI music. It uses an AI detector to label fully AI tracks and excludes that music from algorithmic or editorial recommendation. Plus, as of July, Deezer started removing any AI tracks that it suspects have been used in streaming fraud scams or that haven’t been streamed for six months or more. (Deezer has said previously that up to 85% of the streams on fully AI tracks were fraudulent).

In January, Bandcamp announced a total ban on AI-generated songs on its platform, whether that AI use is in all or a “substantial part” of the creative process. This was a first for online music platforms, but it came a few months after iHeartRadio announced a similar ban on “play[ing] AI music that features synthetic vocalists pretending to be human,” on its radio stations nationwide. Bandcamp also noted that it does not allow the use of any AI tools “to impersonate other artists or styles.”

Now These Apple Services Are Down

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you're having trouble accessing certain Apple products and services today, you are not alone. As of early Tuesday afternoon, Downdetector shows user reports of issues across multiple Apple services. While Apple's System Status page initially showed far fewer issues than Downdetector users reported, the site has since updated to reflect general outages.

According to users on Downdetector, there are currently outages or interruptions to the following Apple services, in order of most to least reported: Apple Support, Apple Pay, iCloud, App Store, Apple Business Manager, Apple Music, Apple Store, Sign In with Apple, Apple Card. As of 1:44 p.m., Apple Support had the most reports, with over 13,000 user complaints. Comments suggest that users cannot log into their iCloud accounts, Apple Accounts, or activate devices, among other issues.

According to Apple, the disruptions aren't quite as widespread: Apple says that the App Store, Apple Music, and AppleCare on Devices are all experiencing outages. Apple says that the App Store and Apple Music "may be slow or unavailable," while AppleCare purchase is currently unavailable. The company's System Status page shows an "issue" with iCloud Account & Sign In. Other services with user reports on Downdetector, including the Apple Card, Apple Online Store, and Apple Business, read as "Available." Apple also says there was an outage with the Volume Purchase Program, but that has since been resolved.

It's not clear what's causing the issues with the App Store, Apple Music, iCloud Account & Sign In, or other Apple services at this time. However, the timing is odd. It comes after a number of banks experienced outages on Monday, as well as Microsoft's massive outage the previous week. In addition to all of these outages, the FAA unexpectedly grounded all U.S. flights on Friday, for only the third time in the agency's history.

As decision model use skyrockets, is customization the next frontier?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In October, more than one in four requests on Vercel’s AI model router went to decision models, not LLMs — an enormous share of work for a kind of computerized intelligence that was invented only a month earlier.

Decision models are LLMs that don’t produce output tokens; instead, they’re tuned to use their general intelligence to quickly and cheaply decide between preset options, while registering its level of confidence in the choice. They’re useful in production environments, where fast decisions can be more important than the reasoning provided by a slower and more expensive frontier model.

TypeSafe became a $7.5 billion company after releasing its Jev family of models, which continues to dominate usage (at least where we can see metrics from services like OpenRouter, which handled more than 1.5 billion queries to TypeSafe’s models last week).

However, it’s easy to make a decision model, and dozens were released in the wake of Jev. One Amazon engineer made one in a weekend, and soon every hyperscaler had its own option. On Tuesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said that his company is rolling out its decision model, Microsoft-Decision-1.1, for wide use on Azure immediately.

The action is happening on both ends of the ecosystem: Sqwish, a U.K.-based AI startup behind a decision model called kettei fat, announced a $3 million pre-seed round today. Co-founders Ushnish Sengupta and Federica Freddi, who met as Cambridge grad students, had been developing small LLMs when Jev arrived on the scene, and they realized their architectures could be repurposed as a powerful decision model.

Data appears to be the key to a successful model in this category — TypeSafe CEO Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch that the hardest part of building Jev was finely calibrating its judgement using statistically-adjusted synthetic data. Sqiwsh, too, has been working with synthetic data, but its unique offering is built-in post-training tools that use a customer’s own data to improve the model’s judgement.

“What makes us not a commodity, I think, is the fact that we are offering our customers the same power…they can keep uploading their production data, and just the same way that we trained our models, their models can also keep getting better at the tasks that they care about,” CEO Ushnish Sengupta told TechCrunch.

Attempting to rank decision models is difficult; Sqwish’s “kettei fat” model has performed well on some benchmarks, but most builders of the technology say that the real proof is in the pudding: it’s generally simple for developers to sub in one model for another and see how it does in a specific context.

“The benchmark is more of an invitation to try the model out, and then the continual tuning is the reason why why they should stick to us,” Sengupta says.

Microsoft’s Decision 1.1 model is also the result of the company carefully balancing across cost, latency, intelligence and calibration. Achint Srivastava, the engineering VP who led the project, agreed that too much focus on benchmarks is a mistake.

“What we see is that ‘benchmaxed’ models fall flat under real enterprise usage very quickly,” Srivastava wrote in an email. “Our testing underscores just how challenging it is to deliver accuracy, reliability, speed and cost efficiency in a single model. Microsoft-Decision-1.1 and Jev are among the few that perform strongly across all four dimensions, even when evaluated on ‘held out’ or unseen benchmarks reflective of real use cases.”

For startups like Sqwish, competition with hyperscalers and frontier labs is an opportunity. Freddi predicts that future systems will see so-called System One intelligence, like decision models, working in concert with System Two intelligence, like frontier LLMs.

“Even in that future, System One is maybe a $10 billion, $50 billion [opportunity], and OpenAI is doing this IPO with you know $1 trillion, $2 trillion market value in mind,” she told TechCrunch. “This is all we care about; whereas for them, this is just a side project.”

Data center slowdown would raise risks for utility investments

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk after the midterm elections, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, according to a report from Orennia.

Utilities could be left holding the bill for billions in infrastructure spending committed in anticipation of artificial intelligence data centers that are delayed, downsized or canceled, industry observers and experts told Utility Dive.

Opposition to data center construction has crossed traditional party lines, while Democratic gains in the midterms are adding pressure on earlier-stage investments across key markets. Together, these dynamics raise the stakes for project approvals and utility cost recovery, they say.

“From a financing perspective, prolonged uncertainty will increase the cost of capital, reduce available leverage and make investors more hesitant to commit significant development dollars before power is secured,” said Andrejka Bernatova, managing partner of investment firm Dynamix Capital Partners, which invests in energy infrastructure.

Even in regions that theoretically have enough capacity to accommodate new large loads, projects may struggle to secure a credible path to power on the timelines they need.

“Interconnection, permitting, and financing all matter, but they are part of the same execution question of whether the team can get reliable power to the site and actually deliver,” Bernatova said. “A customer commitment or a large announced capacity number does not answer that by itself.”

Ultimately, Bernatova expects investors to favor markets that offer certainty around power availability and timelines, potentially eroding the competitive advantage of markets like Texas, which until recently attracted substantial data center investment.

Over the summer, Gov. Greg Abbott, R, announced a pause on new data center interconnections pending an audit of the queue. In the midterms, he lost his bid for reelection to Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who has pledged to maintain pressure on the industry after taking office next year.

Nearly all of the 46 municipal ballot measures tracked by Bloomberg that addressed data center development resulted in restrictions, moratoriums or bans.

That unfavorability extends to a substantial share of the development pipeline. An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, where incoming Democratic governors campaigned on pausing or restricting construction, according to a report from energy analytics firm Orennia that was shared with Utility Dive.

Texas accounts for nearly half of the pre-construction in development in the U.S., so a statewide approval freeze would have an outsized impact on the national buildout, but the “impact of delayed or unrealized data center build-out on power prices varies widely across technologies and markets,” Orennia said.

If 20% of projected data center demand in the Electric Reliability Council of Texas region fails to materialize, solar and wind generators could see their average wholesale revenue fall 38% and 37%, respectively, according to the report. Implied heat rates, a measure of the economics of gas-fired generation relative to fuel costs, could decline 39%. In the same scenario, two-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads could fall below new-build break-even requirements.

In PJM, delaying half of the planned data center buildout by two years could reduce four-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads by 16% and lower solar and wind capture prices and implied heat rates by about 12% through 2030, the modeling suggests.

The election results are unlikely to produce sweeping federal AI legislation but could lead to congressional hearings and greater transparency requirements, according to D.C.-based research and advisory firm Capstone. Its analysts expect much of the policy debate to take place at state utility commissions and local siting boards.

Despite a proliferation of large-load tariffs over the past year, safeguards may still leave customers exposed in the event of a slowdown, some say.

“Existing tariffs and regulatory frameworks likely won’t fully protect customers because they were designed for a different century,” Louisa Eberle, principal at the Regulatory Assistance Project, warned in a written statement.

Eberle also called for reforms to utility business practices “to turn this data center surge into updated infrastructure, cleaner energy and a better deal for average customers.”

The push for lower electricity bills is prompting policymakers to look more closely at how utilities use existing grid infrastructure.

Richard Caperton, vice president of policy at SPAN, a maker of smart electrical panels, anticipates greater emphasis on data center projects that can be served without major new grid investments.

“I’m really thrilled to see governors like Abigail Spanberger [in Virginia] campaign on affordability and then embrace utilization as the solution,” he said.

In April, Gov. Spanberger signed legislation requiring Dominion Energy and Appalachian Power to report how effectively they use existing grid infrastructure. The State Corporation Commission can use those metrics to establish improvement targets and evaluate utility spending.

“Winning candidates who emphasized lowering electricity prices are already looking for ways to deliver on their promises quickly,” Caperton said. “I expect that will lead to more policies and programs that boost grid utilization being ratified and implemented in early 2027. How those policies affect data center development and utility investments will vary by state.”

Stephen Rose, CEO of infrastructure software provider Render Networks, said he expects to see a “pause and necessary shakeout” in data center development.

“Fully permitted projects with real capital will keep moving,” Rose said. “The speculative end takes the hit: developers land-banking without firm power, and the contractors who staffed up for projects that won't break ground.”

Rose cited AEP Ohio's large-load tariff, approved in July 2025, which requires new data centers above 25 MW to pay for at least 85% of requested capacity for up to 12 years, with exit fees and financial guarantees. The utility’s data-center pipeline subsequently fell by more than half from more than 30 GW to about 13.

Rose expressed concern, however, that a broad slowdown wouldn’t affect hyperscalers as much as it would the businesses and people that build data centers. He recalled delays in Louisiana's Broadband Equity, Access and Deployment program in 2025, where service providers bore the costs of equipment and crews while waiting for approvals.

“When timelines stalled, the hit didn't land on big tech balance sheets. It landed on local drilling, civil and fiber contractors,” Rose said. “A data center slowdown hits the same people first, and Texas risks the same fallout.”

Permitting hurdles could make data center developers more selective about where they build and under what terms.

Andy Roehr, managing director in the tax advisory firm Baker Tilly’s energy and infrastructure practice, sees developers as more likely to redirect capital than abandon projects – though that could lead to a winnowing of queues in some jurisdictions.

“A developer is typically evaluating three, four or five sites at any given moment, so when permitting, regulation or local opposition makes one location difficult to read, the project is far more likely to be delayed or redirected than canceled,” Roehr said.

Roehr said states and municipalities competing for projects that can no longer move forward elsewhere could find themselves in a “bidding war.”

"The most interesting thing I am seeing is that these moratoria are creating a developer’s market since they have the assets, tax revenues and jobs that communities want or need,” he continued.

Some developers may shift to “less optimal sites where projects can get done at an effective price point and time-to-market, but the regulatory and legislative environment is less hostile,” he said.

Changes in expected demand could also lead to repricing across the supply chain, from gas turbines and processor racks to construction costs.

Another uncertainty is the possibility of federal permitting reform, which could make it easier to build certain kinds of energy projects.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which advanced in the Senate last week, faces a difficult path to passage following the midterm elections, analysts say.

Democrats are split over the proposal, with some expressing concerns over changes to environmental protections in the bill and urging party leaders to wait until next year when the Democrats will control both the House and Senate to pursue their priorities.

Roehr said in his view, the BAAJA takes an “all of the above” approach and would be as meaningful to solar as to nuclear or natural gas. But what the industry needs most, he said, is stability.

“Developers, investors and host communities need certainty to keep committing capital and local resources to these projects,” Roehr said. “These are 20-, 40-, even 60-year investments, and if the environment keeps whipsawing then jobs don’t appear, tax payments don’t appear and capital moves on — there will be no winners.”

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

Michigan is among the states weighing reforms to rate case planning and utility profits. Experts and stakeholders told Utility Dive what works — and what doesn’t.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

The donation, which advocates called “problematic” and “convenient” for the company, comes to light as federal regulators chosen by President Donald Trump consider whether to allow NextEra to merge with Dominion Energy.

In North America, data centers are boosting the long-term investment case for energy storage and renewables as gas turbine backlogs and rising fuel volatility drive up peaking costs, according to a note from Wood Mackenzie.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Physicists at the Large Hadron Collider have detected a rare, 1-in-10-billion particle interaction that opens a view into the 'zeptoverse,' the smallest distance scale ever measured.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Using the Large Hadron Collider (LHC), the world's largest particle accelerator, scientists have gotten the first glimpse of the "zeptoverse" — the universe as it behaves within less than a hundred-trillionth of a wavelength of visible light, the smallest scale that researchers have explored to date.

The new findings may one day help to answer long-standing cosmic mysteries, such as why there is far more matter than antimatter seen in the universe, and what makes up the dark matter that constitutes about 85% of all matter in the universe, the researchers announced Nov. 3 at a seminar at CERN (the European Organization for Nuclear Research), the organization that operates the LHC. The study, available as a preprint, has not yet been peer reviewed.

At the LHC, located near Geneva at the border of France and Switzerland, researchers were investigating elusive particles called beauty quarks. These subatomic particles are cousins of the standard "up" and "down" quarks that make up the cores of all atoms in the universe.

When beauty quarks are created — in this case, when the LHC smashes atoms together at incredibly high speeds — they live for just one and a half trillionths of a second before they decay. There are a number of ways beauty quarks can break down; researchers hope that analyzing rare forms of these decays might reveal that they might not unfold as expected.

Such outcomes could then shed light on new kinds of physics found only at very tiny scales of zeptometers, or trillionths of a nanometer. (For comparison, a wavelength of visible light ranges from about 380 to 750 nanometers.)

An artist's visualization of a beauty particle decaying into two muons.

By exploring this zeptoverse, scientists hope to discover new particles that have escaped detection until now.

"We're confident there must be new particles to discover, and these could help explain mysteries like dark matter and the existence of matter in the universe," study co-author Harry Cliff, a particle physicist at the University of Cambridge, told Live Science.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"Why haven't we seen these particles yet? One answer, and perhaps the most likely answer, is these new particles only become manifest at very, very short-distance scales. So by probing into the zepto scale, we give ourselves the best chance possible of finding these new particles that could explain these mysteries."

We're now recording in a single year what took 10 years to record in the past.

Now, after hunting for more than four decades, physicists have found the first evidence of the rarest kind of beauty-quark decay seen yet. "Only one in 10 billion beauty particles decay this way," Cliff said.

In this form of decay, technically known as the B0 to mu+ mu− decay, a beauty quark collides with an oppositely charged beauty antiquark. They annihilate each other, generating a pair of smaller particles ‪—‬ a muon and an antimuon. Previous research suggested that this requires a complicated set of interactions, making this kind of decay extraordinarily rare.

An experiment at the LHC called LHCb was specifically designed to study beauty quarks. In 2024, a major upgrade to LHCb made it possible to distinguish different types of particles. Using machine learning techniques, the researchers suppressed huge amounts of background noise, which helped them detect the first evidence of the B0 to mu+ mu− decay.

"We're now recording in a single year what took 10 years to record in the past," Cliff said. "And so we have this massive sample of beauty decays, and that is ultimately what allowed us to see this decay for the first time."

The new results suggest that the way this rare decay happens matches the predictions of the Standard Model of particle physics, the current best description of how the basic building blocks of the universe behave. As such, the researchers have not yet discovered signs of new particles or new physics beyond the Standard Model.

"For me, I think the most exciting thing about this result is the fact that we are probing these unimaginably short distance scales," Cliff said. "Seeing the evidence for this decay begins a new area of research where we can start to make precision measurements that will give us the best chance of finding new physics in the future."

The researchers would now like to upgrade LHCb to fully exploit the improvements to the LHC's luminosity that are currently planned for the 2030s.

"However, there is some doubt over that now because the U.K. recently withdrew funding from the project," Cliff said. "So we are really trying to figure out if we'll be able to continue this type of research."

In an ideal world, "we'll collect many hundreds of these decays in the future, make precise measurements and probe deep into the zeptoverse," Cliff said.

Aston Martin Reveals Fernando Alonso's Dakar Challenger Inspired By A 'Call Of Duty' Tie-In

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 2027 Dakar Rally is already set to be an unusual affair with an unexpected return to Africa as the route shifts from Saudi Arabia to Portugal and Morocco. Now, three Aston Martins will be tackling the famed cross-country rally in January. The British manufacturer revealed the Dreadnought on Tuesday, its first rally-raid machine. If that nameplate sounds familiar, the Aston Martin Dreadnought was originally a digital-only vehicle exclusively designed for "Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4."

The two-time F1 world champion won't be heading out into the desert alone. He will reunite with his previous Dakar co-driver, Marc Coma. Few competitors are as accomplished and experienced as Coma. The 50-year-old won the Dakar five times with KTM and briefly served as the rally's race director between 2016 and 2018. As a pairing, Alonso and Coma finished 13th in the 2020 Dakar Rally with Toyota. Alonso said his return was driven by a desire to challenge himself. Honestly, F1 isn't much of a challenge at Aston Martin this season. The Spaniard is just trundling around, hoping to finish races. He said in a release:

"After deciding to continue racing in Formula One with the Aston Martin Aramco, I thought carefully about Dakar. In the end, the decision became simple. I wanted to embrace the challenge completely and take on both Formula One and the Dakar Rally in the same year."

The version of the Aston Martin Dreadnought won't feature a carbon-fiber chassis, military-grade armor plating or weapons storage like its Call of Duty counterpart. This Dreadnought is based on the Prodrive-designed Dacia Sandrider. The machine is fitted with the same three-liter, twin-turbocharged Nissan engine. The starkest difference is the bodywork, styled after the digital-only combat SUV, most evident in the square headlights and the louvered rear window.

The Prodrive-run effort will also include another usual suspect at the wheel of a Dreadnought: Nine-time World Rally Champion Sébastien Loeb. The living legend has never won the Dakar Rally, but has finished second on three occasions. The third Dreadnought will be driven by Lucas Moraes. The Brazilian joined the Dacia-Prodrive effort after winning the World Rally-Raid Championship with Toyota in 2025. The vehicle's origin may seem ridiculous, but Aston Martin has a legitimate chance of winning the Dakar in January. Prodrive is the returning winner after Nasser Al-Attiyah took the 2026 victory in a Dacia Sandrider.

I’m Worried NBC Is Going To Misunderstand Why The Traitors New Blood Isn't Hitting

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

I’m Confused About A Survivor 51 Player’s Baffling Tribal Council Decision

Jamie Lee Curtis’s New TV Show Is Giving Me All The ‘90s Sitcom Nostalgia, But Now I’m Worried

A Survivor 51 Castaway Is Not Pleased With Jeff Probst And Called Him Out On Social

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We’re only a few days away from the Traitors: New Blood finale, and I’m willing to admit the obvious. This has not been the best season of The Traitors. In fact, you could easily say this has been a below-average season. Fans on social media and those I have talked to in real life seem to all be in agreement on that. There’s a growing consensus that the show needs to change. I think producers are going to listen, though I’m terrified they’re going to misunderstand the why.

The biggest, loudest and most obvious change we’ve gotten this season has been the casting of non-celebrities. The US version of The Traitors has historically relied on reality television personalities to provide the drama and bring their fanbases. This season, NBC decided to move the property from Peacock to network television and cast it like a more traditional competition show with randoms. It would be easy to just look at that decision and blame all the problems on the newbies, but that’s actually not the right takeaway.

The biggest problem by a very large margin with Traitors: New Blood is the episode runtimes. The show simply does not work in an hour-long time slot on NBC. In theory, the episodes on Peacock are the same length, as they’re typically between an hour and an hour and ten minutes. Because they’re on streaming, however, that runtime is the actual length of each episode. On network television, an hour-long time slot is actually forty-two minutes or so of real content, which isn’t even close to the amount of time the players need to complete one cycle of the game.

That means every single episode, the editors are making an impossible choice. They can either not include one or more major elements of the show (the breakfast, the mission, the roundtable and the turret), they can completely remove all of the strategy scenes that happen between those major elements, or they can drop most of the confessionals where the players talk directly to the camera and tell us their motivations and theories. There is simply no way to get through all of that in forty-two minutes. So, that has left every episode feeling either incomplete because it didn’t make it through a full cycle of the game, or feeling like a highlight video that simply recaps the basics of what happened.

That format and structure are the problem. The runtime is the problem. It’s not the cast. Even with less confessionals and less screentime, we’ve gotten good content from these players. I’m not saying the Traitors: New Blood cast is better than the cast on Big Brother 14 or Survivor: Heroes vs Villains, but there are some breakout personalities here and plenty of likeable, solid characters who have popped and would have looked even better with more of a chance to talk to the camera.

Victor is an all-time great Traitors character and should be a reality television mainstay moving forward. He’s electric. I’m personally obsessed with Jay and have had probably ten conversations with people trying to figure out his vibe and what’s going on in his head. It’s not just those two standouts either. There are a half dozen or so other players who play hard and are capable of delivering strong confessionals. Joe, Katie, Tomica, Michael and Shane have all had great moments, and when given a chance, secondary characters like Wyatt, Clyde, Abby, Abbey, Kriste, Ben, Morgan and others have delivered too. They’re just not always getting the screentime because, once again, every episode feels like it’s set on 1.5X speed.

Survivor and The Amazing Race have lengthened their episodes in recent years to help develop the characters and let the episodes breathe more. That’s the obvious solution here for The Traitors. It either needs to move to 90 minutes, or it needs to move back to Peacock. What it absolutely does not need to do is give up on casting newbies. The UK and other countries have had fantastic seasons using randoms, and in an ideal world, it would be a delight to watch Peacock/ NBC switch back and forth between a season with reality stars and a season with plumbers, farmers and firefighters.

I love The Traitors. It’s one of my favorite shows on television, and despite the obvious format issues, I still enjoyed the content we got this season. I’d like to see producers listen to the fan frustration and make a change, but those alterations should be exclusively focused on the format and not on casting.

Pedestal AI raises $4.7 million to agentify the supply chain

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Delivery supply chains are the lifeblood of contemporary businesses — and a new startup is betting that purpose-built AI agents can make them run a lot more smoothly.

On Tuesday, Pedestal AI emerged from stealth with $4.7 million in seed funding. The company’s goal is to use agentic AI to make supply chains faster and more reliable — a plan that’s already brought them together with Fortune 500 companies and some of the largest shipping firms in the world.

The seed round was led by Eclipse, with participation from RRE, Converge VC, Timescale Ventures, and VitalStage. Pedestal says the funding will be used to expand its product and engineering time, grow the marketing organization, and support additional deployments.

In practice, much of Pedestal’s work is about removing friction from the shipping process — with agentic technology used as a means to that end. In one case, the company reduced missed deliveries for a major furniture company by 20% by deploying agents to take on some of the work that drivers were supposed to do. The agentic system they built would call customers 30 minutes before a delivery and collect any special instructions; if the call didn’t go through, a text would be sent from a separate system. Along the way, the deployment collected a wealth of data about friction points in the process, which could be used to optimize the system even further.

CEO Raj Aggarwal says contemporary AI systems are particularly useful for dealing with that data. “The challenge is that the data is messy and it’s constantly changing,” Aggarwal told TechCrunch. “It’s disparate because it sits in so many legacy systems. That’s what makes it hard.”

Supply chain AI has become a particularly hot space in recent months as alumni from autonomous driving teams look to launch their own startups. The supply chain startup Atomic, launched by a team of Tesla alumni, came out of stealth in 2025 with an emphasis on streamlining inventory. Another venture called Loop was launched by Uber alums to focus on predictive analytics.

As Aggarwal sees it, deliveries are particularly well-suited to training reinforcement learning systems. Because of their high volumes and easily measured outcomes, it’s easy to fine-tune contemporary AI systems toward what’s working and away from what isn’t.

Perhaps most importantly, it’s the kind of problem that can have an immediate effect on a client’s bottom line, making it easy to justify the expense.

“If you can get it right,” he told TechCrunch, “you can have really big impact.”

The Nvidia Scandal Could Upend Chip Sales to China. Trump Wants to Keep Them Going

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After days of political fallout from former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, President Donald Trump is defending commerce secretary Howard Lutnick.

Last week, Lutnick came under fire from treasury secretary Scott Bessent for not aggressively imposing export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

But Trump’s defense of Lutnick, sources tell WIRED, reflects the president's own view that allowing the sale of some advanced AI chips to China can keep China reliant on US technology and simultaneously boost US tech stocks.

Lutnick “was doing that because if you didn’t delay it, if you didn’t do something, you could have a blowup with China. We’re having a very good relationship with China right now,” Trump said ahead of a multiday trip to Asia and a meeting on Thursday with Chinese leader Xi Jinping.

Trump convened an Oval Office meeting on November 5 to discuss the political fallout from Huang’s affair with Alice Huang, the former Megaspeed executive whom some in the US government believe was recruited to spy for China, according to two sources briefed on the discussion.

Megaspeed received advanced AI chips manufactured by Nvidia through several layers of companies that started with a subsidiary of a blacklisted Chinese server conglomerate called Inspur, The New York Times has reported.

In the meeting, the sources say, Bessent confronted Lutnick, asking why Megaspeed was not added to the entity list faster, given that secretary of state Marco Rubio and US trade representative Jamieson Greer, among others, had already agreed it was the correct move.

Lutnick responded that he would defer to Trump and would add Megaspeed to the entity list if that was what the president wanted, the sources say.

But Trump settled on a third approach: for Lutnick to work on introducing a new interim trade rule to stop Chinese AI labs from using advanced AI chips remotely by leasing their compute power from data centers in Southeast Asia.

In a statement to WIRED, a White House spokesperson said the new rule would “crack down on the smuggling of advanced US SI chips” and “help ensure that America’s most advanced chips go first to US SI innovators, not Chinese shell companies.” (Superintelligence, or SI, is the administration’s term for artificial intelligence.)

A senior White House official added that Trump has been adamant that Chinese firms cannot acquire the most advanced Nvidia chips, known as Blackwells and Rubins. Those are different from the H200-type chips, built on previous-generation Hopper architecture, that Megaspeed was acquiring.

Lutnick’s position appears to have been based on a series of considerations, the sources say. For one, Megaspeed has already had the chips for months, and blacklisting the company would be akin to playing whack-a-mole unless the administration dealt with the underlying issue of Chinese firms getting remote access to US chips.

Meanwhile, Lutnick has moved cautiously on any new export restrictions—essentially waiting to get explicit approval from Trump—after he faced heavy criticism in 2025 for expanding restrictions against firms with Chinese ties, the sources said.

In September 2025, the Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security started aggressively using the so-called affiliates rule, which automatically extends trade restrictions to any company that is 50 percent owned by a company already on the US entity list or the military end-user list.

But the move prompted immediate retaliation by China, which suspended exports of critical rare earth metals to the US and, the sources said, prompted a wave of internal criticism accusing the Commerce Department of having sparked a trade war.

After the Busan summit at the end of October 2025, where Trump and Chinese president Xi Jinping agreed to a trade truce, the White House instructed Commerce to halt the use of the affiliates rule and clear new export controls in advance, the sources said.

The suspension of the affiliates rule—which came almost as soon as it was implemented—may have had a knock-on effect on companies like Megaspeed, one person familiar with deliberations said.

Inspur, the Chinese server conglomerate connected to Megaspeed, operated a subsidiary called Aivres, which, as a California-based company, could legally buy restricted Nvidia chips without triggering direct export bans and ship them internationally to Southeast Asia.

In total, Aivres transferred billions of dollars’ worth of restricted Nvidia chips, including the powerful H200 chips, to Megaspeed’s subsidiaries.

Instead of shipping the physical chips into China, Megaspeed hosted servers at data centers in Malaysia and Indonesia—then rented out compute power to major Chinese companies. A spokesperson for Aivres said in a statement that the company complies with all US export controls.

Date: Confirmed, Rating: Meets Expectations, Comments: None via @sejournal, @pedrodias

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Google's core updates arrive with a start time and a scope. Everything else you've read about them came from somewhere else.

The routine is familiar by now. Google posts a sentence. It has a name, a start time, and a note that the rollout may take up to two weeks. Within the hour, somebody has published a “what we know so far.” By the evening, there’s a list of winners and losers. Before the week is out, with the rollout still running, there’s a recovery guide in seven steps.

I’ve watched this for most of 20 years. The only part that has changed is the sentence. It used to be longer.

When Google launched what the industry later called Panda, in February 2011, they wrote a post about it. The post said what the change was for: sites that add little, copy from others, or just aren’t very useful. It said how big it was: 11.8% of queries. You could argue with the result, and plenty did, but there was a stated target to argue with.

Here is the entry for the November 2026 core update, which started on Nov. 10. It was released. It applies globally and in all languages. It may take up to two weeks. That’s the lot: three facts, none of them about your site.

Google has been open about why. In a Q&A on Search updates, it said it changes Search thousands of times a year and announces the ones it considers notable, about 10 a year when it last counted in public. Its core updates documentation adds that smaller core updates go out continually and aren’t announced at all. So a named update is a ranking change that Google decided to put on a calendar. The calendar entry tells you when. It tells you nothing about what changed, or about what happens in between.

The announcement shrank to a timestamp, and the commentary expanded to fill the space.

The date does have a use. Google’s own guidance uses it: wait a week after the rollout finishes, then compare that week with the week before it started. The date draws a line through your data so you know where to look. Visibility trackers do a similar job across the market; they show which sites moved.

What neither of them shows is why. Which system changed, and why it moved your site in particular, is known inside Google and nowhere else. A rank tracker and a strong feeling won’t close that gap.

That hasn’t slowed anyone down. An obscure tool vendor published a winners-and-losers study of the November update within a day or two of the launch. It gave the wrong start date and called it the sixth core update of the year. Google’s dashboard lists three. I’d say the analysis was rushed, but that implies there was something to analyze.

The closest thing I know to a core update is the annual performance review.

Anyone who has had a job knows the meeting. You walk in and get a rating that was settled long before the meeting was booked. It reflects the year you had. It also reflects the people you were measured against, because plenty of companies calibrate ratings across a team; you can do the same work as last year and drop a grade because three colleagues did better. Search works the same way. A page is ranked against every other page that could answer the query, so a site can lose ground without changing anything.

A core update is review day. It’s when the rating becomes visible. Nobody sensible leaves that meeting and asks what changed about the meeting.

The comparison stops in one place. After a review you get a manager, written feedback and a chance to ask what went wrong. After a core update you get a dashboard entry. That missing conversation is the entire market for update commentary: an industry of people offering to be the manager who explains your rating, none of whom were in the calibration room.

The strongest objection to all this is that the losses are real, and it’s correct. Companies do lose a large share of their organic revenue overnight, and people lose jobs over it. The practitioners who spend their time on recoveries are dealing with real businesses in real trouble.

There’s also evidence that sometimes the reviewer gets it wrong. In October 2024, Google invited a group of independent publishers hit by the September 2023 helpful content update to its campus. According to people who were in the room, they were told their content wasn’t the problem, and that they shouldn’t count on getting their traffic back. That is an awkward fact for anyone, me included, who says most affected sites had it coming.

Look at what it does to the recovery guide, though. If the content wasn’t the issue, “seven steps for improving your content” were never going to work. The publishers with the best case against Google are also the best evidence that nobody outside it could diagnose them.

Now the less sympathetic group, which in my experience is the bigger one.

Most of the affected sites I’ve been asked to look at over 20 years had the same history. Somebody decided what they could get away with before finding out what the market already offered. They hadn’t researched what customers needed or looked at how good the existing answers were. They picked keywords with volume, wrote about them, and launched.

When that didn’t work, they went looking for a shortcut, and social media had plenty. A thread with a promise that was too good to be true, and was. A “hack” from somebody who discovered SEO in the summer and by autumn had a conference slot and a YouTube channel. In office terms, that’s career advice from the colleague who joined in August and already runs a workshop. They copied it without asking why it would work. Some of these tricks do work for a few months, which is the worst outcome available, because the fall then lands inside an update window and gets blamed on the update.

Then they hired SEO to make it work, as if rankings were a delivery service for businesses that forgot to have a product. I’ve written about the wall that approach runs into; it hasn’t moved.

This is the employee who did the minimum all year and arrives at the review certain that the rating system has changed, and has brought printouts. In fairness to them, they have a problem that needs solving. “The algorithm changed” is something you can say to a board. “We built a site with nothing to say and then paid someone to get it found” tends not to make the deck.

So there’s a buyer for an outside cause, and the update coverage supplies one. It comes with a diagnosis nobody outside Google could have made and a retainer that renews before the next update can contradict it. The seller gets a client, and the client gets a story. The site stays exactly as useful as it was, which was the problem.

This trade feels legitimate to the people in it because of how the industry thinks about quality.

Take E-E-A-T. Google coined it for its quality raters, and Google’s own documentation says it isn’t a specific ranking factor. The industry read that and sold E-E-A-T audits anyway, with scores for a thing Google says it has no score for. Somewhere there is an author bio that was added to fix a business model.

The traits themselves are older than the initialism. Peter Morville’s user experience honeycomb had “credible,” “useful,” and “valuable” in it in 2004, as facets of a product, with no ranking attached. The industry needed Google to hand it an initialism before it noticed that people prefer things they can trust.

“JavaScript SEO” has the same shape: it’s progressive enhancement, which web developers were practicing before anyone gave it a conference track. I made that case in Stop Learning SEO and won’t repeat it.

If quality is a list of items to tick, then a core update is a new edition of the list, and you need someone to tell you what’s on it. There is never a shortage of volunteers. Back in the office, that’s working to the KPIs. The person who works to the KPIs has to know the moment they change. The person who is good at the job mostly finds out on review day that they still are.

So people who hold quality as a checklist need each update explained; the announcement explains nothing, and so somebody makes one up.

The first question a business could ask after an update is “What did this update change for my site?” Answering it would take the list of systems that changed, how much each one counts, and what every competing page did in the same fortnight. Nobody outside Google has those. You can still pay for an answer. It’ll arrive as a confident PDF with a traffic-light table, and it will tell you to improve content quality, show more expertise, and prune the thin pages.

Now read the PDF from the update before. It says the same thing. So will the one after the next update, because the advice was never derived from the update. If the remedy is the same whatever changed, the change taught nobody anything.

The second question is “what do the people I want as customers need that they can’t already get?” It’s a worse question to be asked. It has no villain and no deadline; it can’t be handed to a thread, and the answer may be that the business has nothing to add yet. It’s also slower and more expensive to answer than a PDF. But it can be answered by people you can find and talk to, and it could have been answered before the site launched.

The fair objection to the second question is Google. Google is in the business of taking in what other people know and serving it back as its own answer, and AI Overviews and AI Mode have made that the main event. The part of the web where people found you through a search result is being eaten, and Google is the one holding the fork. I’ve covered what that did to publishers’ traffic. So why polish anything for a channel that’s shrinking?

Because the second question was never about the channel. A site is a product, and what people need from it doesn’t change when fewer of them arrive through Google. What does change is how much of what you know you should hand over for free. I’ve argued that publishing everything is a terrible business strategy, and I still think so. Holding back the method is sensible. Shipping a site that’s a bare outline of what you’re supposed to be good at is a different decision, and it fails the customer long before it fails a ranking system.

The line between those two is thin, and it’s meant to be. It sits in a different place for every business, and no update, checklist, or thread will tell you where yours is. A search engine certainly won’t; it looks after its own interests, as it always has. The only people who know how much is enough are the ones you’re trying to sell to, which is one more reason to go and ask them.

In the office, it’s the gap between “what did the calibration committee change this year?” and “what did I do this year?” Only one of those is yours to find out, and it’s the one that doesn’t come with somebody else to blame.

Google will post another sentence soon enough. The recovery guide for it already exists. It’s the one from last time.

Sen. John Kennedy Worries Outages Could Be Cyberattack, Tommy Tuberville Isn't Concerned

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tommy Tuberville isn't losing sleep over the wave of tech outages sweeping the country ... but fellow Republican Sen. John Kennedy suspects hackers could be behind the disruptions ... and he's demanding answers.

Charlie caught up with Alabama's governor-elect in D.C. Tuesday ... and Tuberville brushed off concerns about the recent outages affecting banks, airlines and other industries, saying, "Let's don't make a mountain out of a molehill" ... insisting Trump has things under control.

Kennedy, meanwhile, had a much different take when Charlie caught up with him ... suggesting hackers could be exploiting artificial intelligence to cause the disruptions.

The Louisiana senator stressed he has no evidence the outages are coordinated ... but said he believes intelligence officials know more than they're letting on and should be briefing Congress.

Tuberville also pushed for privatizing air traffic control ... arguing the government needs to get out of the way and let private citizens run things.

And Kennedy had one more piece of advice for anyone worried about the outages ... stock up on WD-40, duct tape and Vienna sausages. He joked you could live forever on those three, plus water.

The comments come after a string of disruptions involving Microsoft, the FAA, airlines and banks ... though there's no confirmed evidence the outages are connected or part of a coordinated cyberattack

Whale positioning has turned positive after four months, but the hacker-linked wallet's $136.8 million ETH conversion adds uncertainty to market sentiment.

ETH needs to reclaim $1,829.77 to strengthen recovery prospects, while losing $1,725 could expose the $1,500–$1,540 demand zone.

The Ethereum price has been facing tremendous selling pressure ever since it broke the crucial support at $2,225. The US midterm election results, the Binance hack, the AI-bubble scare and the latest NVIDIA shock have dragged the price lower by more than 20%. Currently, the price is struggling to recover following a breakdown below $2,000, which has pushed the token close to $1,800.

However, the market is showing an interesting shift beneath the surface: while retail traders have faced a wave of liquidation, large investors appear to be positioning themselves for a potential recovery. This combination makes the next move extremely important as the ETH price continues to consolidate around a crucial support zone.

One of the more interesting developments is the change in Ethereum’s whale vs retail delta. The indicator has spent nearly four months in negative territory, suggesting that whale positioning was weaker relative to retail participation during that period. However, the latest readings show a sharp reversal, with the metric moving back into positive territory and climbing rapidly.

This shift comes as Ethereum’s recent price decline has triggered widespread liquidations among retail traders holding long positions. While small traders have been forced out of their positions, larger market participants appear to be increasing their exposure. This divergence is worth watching, as large investors continue accumulating while retail traders reduce their exposure; ETH could gradually find stronger buying support.

After reaching local highs around $2,800, the ETH price entered a sustained decline, forming lower highs and lower lows as sellers gradually pushed it towards the $1,800 region. The latest sell-off brought ETH to an intraday low of $1,786.61 before the token recovered to $1,821. However, the rebound remains limited, with price still struggling to reclaim the $1,829.77 resistance level.

The CVD suggests aggressive selling since the start of the month, while the RSI has fallen below the lower threshold, validating the bearish claim. The immediate support to watch sits around $1,725, and a daily close below this level could expose ETH to further losses. This could further bring $1,500 to $1,540 demand into focus, which has previously attracted buyers and could become important if the current correction continues.

On the upside, ETH needs to reclaim $1,829.77 to improve its immediate outlook. A sustained move above this level could provide room for a recovery towards the 1,900–2,000 region, where the price previously consolidated.

The Ethereum price is showing a possible change in investor positioning, with the whale vs retail delta turning positive after nearly four months of negative readings. This suggests that larger participants may be taking advantage of the recent correction while retail traders are being forced out of leveraged long positions.

However, the movement of 74,880 ETH from the Binance hacker-linked wallet through THORChain adds another layer of uncertainty, particularly if further conversions follow.

The $1,725 support level remains crucial for ETH’s near-term outlook. Holding it could allow buyers to build on the improving whale-positioning signal, while a breakdown could expose the lower 1,500–1,540 demand zone. Until ETH reclaims $1,829.77, however, a sustained recovery remains unconfirmed.

Right, I've had it. I have been rolled out like bread dough by errant minivans one too many times, and I've decided to do something about it. I am abolishing cars (within a very specific radius, sometimes).

Cycletopia – Replace cars with bicycles is a Grand Theft Auto 5 mod that does the thing it says in its title, which I wrote just there. All cars within a certain radius (I never figured out what radius, exactly, but there was definitely one involved) will, at the touch of a button, transmogrify into bicycles.

They will also, quite often, take flight, owing to some confusion regarding the laws of physics, leading to a hundred different iterations of that one bit from ET.

They are also all ridden by Franklin, since the button to turn on Bike World is the same button that turns the world's pedestrians into carbon copies of your player character. You'll tap the button and, nary moments later, hear "Aw shit!" as your doppelganger gaily endos down the freeway.

It is a nicer, quieter city. Gone are the vrrms of engines and the kablooeys of explosions that now make up the aural world of my modded Los Santos. In their place is the whirr of pedals and the occasional bell, with some allowances having to be made for Franklin's commentary, which is frequent and ribald. I hope this is what GTA 6 is like. Probably it will be.

GTA 5 art director: "Let what's best for the game dictate the way forward"

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Aaron Garbutt explains what Rockstar learned to help shape GTA 5

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 studio head Aaron Garbutt said that the most valuable lesson Rockstar learned ahead of GTA 5 was to kill its darlings, and "let what's best for the game dictate the way forward."

Speaking to Edge magazine back in 2013, Aaron Garbutt, who served as art director on GTA 5, was asked what the most valuable lessons Rockstar had learned from earlier Grand Theft Auto games had been. In response, he said that it was "mostly not to get locked down to anything, whether it's a process, a plan, a goal, an asset, an idea - anything."

Garbutt said the key was "to keep completely open-minded, adapt to the situation and always look for a better way forward, rather than stick with what's been decided previously. I don't mean chop and change for the sake of it, but let what's best for the game dictate the way forward. With a game that's as large and complex as this, the ideas need to be fluid, and you need to be paying as much attention as you can to every aspect to make sure they're working together."

His philosophy isn't exactly a brand-new one. The idea of killing your darlings - abandoning even your very favorite ideas if you find they no longer actually serve the project you're working on - has long been touted across the entire artistic industry. But applied to the complexity of a game like GTA 5, and its then-industry-leading open-world, the idea of knowing when to move on takes on increased importance.

Even back in 2013, Garbutt acknowledged that Rockstar was lucky to have an art team "that is extremely experienced at building open worlds," and was rarely stumped by brand-new problems. Even so, "there's always new things that come along [...] and the nature of how we approach development means that we need to be ready to adapt." For Rockstar, that's a layering approach, where the world is built first, and then missions, story, and game structure are added on top. "Each of these things influences the others massively," Garbutt said, "so each needs to adapt to the others. We are always playing and refining, adjusting each element of the game to play better with the others."

That approach puts Rockstar in a spot where even when it does do away with ideas that aren't working "we're rarely throwing away content." Instead, plans are adapted, and the studio is "constantly looking ahead with a clean slate, rather than sticking stubornly to old decisions."

It sounds basic, but Garbutt's idea here is a pivotal one in a medium as complex as game design. There's a though that few, if any, other forms of art are trying as hard to not get made as video games are, and when some ideas work well with certain features but clash with others, or take ages to actually implement (often at the expense of other systems), reworking them, or even abandoning them entirely, can be an important way to actually get projects finished on time. Sometimes that means that the grand ambitions of the developers don't get entirely delivered on, but if that means getting the game into players hands - or even avoiding having your studio shuttered around you - it's an important discipline to hone.

Classic Comedy Performances From Dave Chappelle, Chris Rock, Bob Newhart and More Headed to Streaming

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Gorilla Comedy+ is launching 'From the Archives' on Nov. 23, featuring collections of legendary comedians and their iconic routines.

Classic comedy performances from the likes of Dave Chappelle, Chris Rock, Bob Newhart and more are headed to audiences this month via a new collection from Gorilla Comedy+.

Gorilla Comedy+, the premium streaming destination dedicated entirely to stand-up comedy from independent comedy media company 800 Pound Gorilla Media, will launch From The Archives, a new collection “celebrating the comedians and performances that helped shape the art form” rolling out over the next two months. The collection “will give audiences the opportunity to revisit landmark stand-up performances, rediscover beloved comedy legends and experience classic material through a more accessible streaming destination,” according to Gorilla Comedy+.
